# Node reference

Generated from the node registry metadata (`listNodeTypes()`) by `node scripts/gen-node-reference.mjs` — do not edit by hand. The same metadata, machine-readable, is in [nodes.json](./nodes.json). For the graph JSON format and field-expression grammar see [authoring.md](./authoring.md).

77 node types, grouped by `category` (node sections below are alphabetical):

**attribute**

- [attributeReduce](#attributereduce) — Collapses a numeric attribute over a whole domain into a single value on the detail domain: sum, min, max, average (all f32, componentwise, keeping the source tuple size) or count (u32, the number of elements — this mode reads no attribute).
- [attributeRemap](#attributeremap) — Rescales a numeric attribute linearly from an input range to an output range, writing f32.
- [brickConnectivity](#brickconnectivity) — Reads BRICKS on a stud lattice — one per point, its world corners in two point attributes, as packBricks writes them — and says how they hold together: which clutch-connected piece each brick belongs to, and the build STEP at which it can be placed.
- [partitionByAttribute](#partitionbyattribute) — Splits the input into one point cloud per distinct value of an i32, u32, or string point attribute (tuple 1).
- [pathCoverage](#pathcoverage) — Measures, at every point of the `path` input, whether that point is COVERED from some direction by any of the oriented boxes in the `boxes` input, and writes the answer as point attributes: coveredAttr gets a bool (did at least minHits rays hit anything), hitsAttr gets the u32 count of rays that did.
- [pathRuns](#pathruns) — Writes a SEGMENTED running total of a numeric point attribute along every polyline: like pathScan, except the accumulator RESETS at points a boolean attribute flags, and on a closed path a run may cross the seam.
- [pathScan](#pathscan) — Writes a RUNNING FOLD of a numeric point attribute along every polyline, in the path's own walk order — by default a prefix SUM, the accumulating counterpart to attributeReduce's collapse, and under `reduce` a running minimum or maximum over that same walk instead.
- [pathShift](#pathshift) — Reads one or more of a path's OWN point attributes from the point `offset` positions further along the polyline's walk order, and writes each into a column of its own — LEAD AND LAG on a path.
- [pointNeighborhood](#pointneighborhood) — Measures each point's neighborhood inside the same cloud and writes the result as point attributes: countAttr receives how many other points lie within radius (u32), and averageAttr/averageOutAttr average a numeric point attribute over those neighbors (f32, same tuple size — averaging "P" gives each point the centroid of its neighbors, which is one Lloyd relaxation step away from even spacing).
- [promoteAttribute](#promoteattribute) — Moves an attribute between domains using the geometry's topology, creating or overwriting it on the target domain.
- [quotaRebalance](#quotarebalance) — Reads a CATEGORY per point and a stated SHARE BAND per category, and writes down the minimum set of points that must change category for every share to land inside its band — which category each should join, and nothing else.
- [removeAttribute](#removeattribute) — Deletes named attributes from one domain.
- [runFit](#runfit) — Cuts every polyline into RUNS at along-arc GAPS, least-squares fits a numeric point attribute against arc position WITHIN each run, and writes the fit back onto every point of the run it belongs to.
- [sampleNearestPoint](#samplenearestpoint) — For every point of `in`, finds the nearest point of the `source` cloud in 3D (positions from P, ties resolved toward the lowest source index) and records what it found on the output's point domain: distanceAttr gets the distance (f32), indexAttr the source point index (i32), and `attribute`/`outAttribute` copy one of the source's point attributes across.
- [setAttribute](#setattribute) — Creates or overwrites an attribute on the chosen domain.
- [transferAlongPath](#transferalongpath) — Reads a path's point attributes AT ARBITRARY ARC POSITIONS and writes them onto a second, independent point cloud — N stations against an M-point path, with N and M unrelated.
- [transferAttribute](#transferattribute) — Transfers an attribute from the `source` geometry onto the main input's points, creating or overwriting it on the output's point domain.
- [transferByIndex](#transferbyindex) — Reads the `source` geometry's point attributes AT A PER-POINT COMPUTED INDEX and writes them onto the main input's points, creating or overwriting them on the output's point domain.
- [writeCurveFrame](#writecurveframe) — Writes a full orthonormal frame — `tangent`, `curveNormal` and `curveBinormal` (f32 tuple 3) — at the points of every polyline primitive, keeping the points, their attributes and the topology exactly as they arrived.
- [writeTangents](#writetangents) — Writes a unit `tangent` (f32 tuple 3) onto the points of every polyline primitive, keeping the points, their attributes and the topology exactly as they arrived — the output is still a path.

**composite**

- [applyRules](#applyrules) — Composite node that runs a REWRITING GRAMMAR over labelled cells, one ROUND at a time: the recursion of a split grammar (a facade into floors, floors into panels, panels halved until they are small), in a graph that cannot wire a cycle.
- [forEach](#foreach) — Composite node that cooks an inner graph ONCE PER ELEMENT instead of once.
- [repeatUntil](#repeatuntil) — Composite node that cooks an inner graph REPEATEDLY, feeding each round's output back into its own input until the body stops changing anything — a bounded fixed point, in a graph where a cycle cannot be wired.
- [subgraph](#subgraph) — Composite node wrapping an inner graph as a single node.

**filter**

- [filterByAttribute](#filterbyattribute) — Keeps points whose named point attribute satisfies a comparison.
- [filterByBounds](#filterbybounds) — Keeps points by position against the axis-aligned box [boundsMin, boundsMax].
- [filterByDensity](#filterbydensity) — Filters points by their `density` point attribute (f32, tuple 1).
- [filterByExpression](#filterbyexpression) — Keeps points where a field-capable `predicate` evaluates to a non-zero number.
- [filterPrimitivesByAttribute](#filterprimitivesbyattribute) — Keeps WHOLE PRIMITIVES whose named PRIMITIVE attribute satisfies a comparison, and preserves topology: the survivors keep their vertices, their vertex and primitive attributes, and the points they share, so a network that goes in comes out a network.
- [filterPrimitivesByBounds](#filterprimitivesbybounds) — Keeps or drops WHOLE PRIMITIVES by testing their vertices against the axis-aligned box [boundsMin, boundsMax], and it is one of the two filters in this library that PRESERVE TOPOLOGY (filterPrimitivesByAttribute, which tests a value a primitive carries rather than where its vertices lie, is the other): the survivors keep their vertices, their vertex and primitive attributes, and the points they share, so a network that goes in comes out a network.
- [occlusionCull](#occlusioncull) — Removes points whose ORIENTED BOX stands in a line of sight, after first trying to move them out of it.
- [pathClip](#pathclip) — CUTS every polyline where `field` changes sign and keeps the pieces where it is >= 0, as polylines of their own — the path node that changes TOPOLOGY by value.
- [projectToPlane](#projecttoplane) — Projects every point orthogonally onto the plane through `origin` with normal `normal` (normalized internally).
- [selfPrune](#selfprune) — Enforces a minimum distance between points, under one of two rules chosen by `mode`.

**io**

- [dataInput](#datainput) — Emits exactly the data items in its `items` param, unchanged — the bridge for injecting externally produced data (for example parent-cell outputs in the hierarchical runtime) into a graph.

**point op**

- [boxContact](#boxcontact) — Asks, for every BOX on the `in` pin (the QUERIES), whether it meets any box on the `targets` pin, and writes the answer onto the query points as attributes: `hitAttr` a bool (any target met), `countAttr` an i32 (how many distinct targets), `targetAttr` an i32 (the index of the met target whose centre is nearest the centre of the query box as tested, -1 for none).
- [connectByIndex](#connectbyindex) — Builds one 2-vertex `polyline` per point, from that point to the point its `index` names — the edge set a PARENT POINTER describes.
- [connectPoints](#connectpoints) — Connects a point cloud into a NETWORK: one 2-vertex `polyline` primitive per edge, built over the SAME points that arrived, so every point attribute survives and a junction is genuinely one point shared by every edge that meets there.
- [copyToPoints](#copytopoints) — Copies the source point cloud onto every target point (output count = source points * target points, grouped by target).
- [jitterPoints](#jitterpoints) — Offsets each point by a deterministic random vector: each axis moves by a uniform random in [-amount, +amount], hashed from (seed, point IDENTITY, axis).
- [mergePoints](#mergepoints) — Concatenates the points of every connected geometry, in connection order, into one point cloud.
- [mergePrimitives](#mergeprimitives) — Concatenates every connected geometry, in connection order, KEEPING TOPOLOGY: points, vertices and primitives are appended and each input's vertex and primitive references are renumbered onto its place in the result, so an authored network merged with a generated one comes out a single network.
- [offsetBox](#offsetbox) — MOVES THE FACES of every cell's box OUT, or IN where the offset is negative — the scope offset of a shape grammar, the padding of a bounding box, the inset/outset of a face extrude — and changes nothing else: same points, same order, same seeds, every attribute and the topology carried, only `boundsMin`/`boundsMax` rewritten.
- [orientAlongVector](#orientalongvector) — Sets the standard rot point attribute (f32 tuple 4 quaternion, [x, y, z, w]) so the chosen local axis points along `direction`, with `up` fixing the roll.
- [packBricks](#packbricks) — Reads axis-aligned BOXES on a stud lattice — one per point, its corners in two point attributes — and writes the BRICKS that build them: one point per brick, from a stated catalogue of footprints, that together fill exactly the lattice cells the boxes cover.
- [pathPointAt](#pathpointat) — Moves every point of every polyline to the position at a given parameter ALONG ITS OWN polyline, and writes the unit `tangent` and `curveU` it finds there.
- [placeBox](#placebox) — PLACES ONE BOX INSIDE, OR AGAINST, every cell's own box, by SIZE, ANCHOR and OFFSET on each axis, and writes it as the cell's new box: the scope operations of a shape grammar (size the scope, centre it, move it in its own frame) with a per-axis min / centre / max justify.
- [pointsToPath](#pointstopath) — Turns a point cloud into one or more paths by building `polyline` primitives over the SAME points, so every point attribute survives — this is the only way to produce a path from a serialized graph.
- [repeatPoints](#repeatpoints) — Emits every input point `count` times — a PER-POINT number of times when `count` is a field — and numbers the copies 0, 1, ..., count - 1 in `indexAttr`.
- [setBounds](#setbounds) — Sets the standard per-point bounds attributes: writes boundsMin and boundsMax (f32 tuple 3, world units) on every point, creating the attributes when missing.
- [shellSplit](#shellsplit) — BREAKS every cell's box into its SHELL — floor, the four walls south / north / west / east, and ceiling — plus, if asked, the INTERIOR they enclose, and emits one point per piece, each piece a box with its OWN FRAME and a label: the component split of a split grammar, which turns a mass into the faces later rules decorate.
- [splitBox](#splitbox) — CUTS every cell's box along one axis into an ordered list of LABELLED PIECES and emits one point per piece — the split of a split grammar (a block into storeys, a wall into bays, a bay into jamb | opening | jamb).
- [transformPoints](#transformpoints) — Transforms every point: P' = R * (scale * P) + translate, with R from rotateEuler (degrees, extrinsic XYZ order — world X applied first, then world Y, then world Z; equivalent to intrinsic ZYX, three.js Euler order 'ZYX').
- [voronoiCells](#voronoicells) — Turns a point cloud into its VORONOI DIAGRAM: one closed `polyline` primitive per site, bounding the ground that site is nearer to than any other.

**sampler**

- [arcTile](#arctile) — Lays a REPEATED PIECE over arc ranges of a path: one oriented instance point every `spacing` units between each range's start and end, ready for spawnInstances.
- [pathResample](#pathresample) — Resamples every polyline primitive at even arc-length steps and emits a PATH, not a cloud: the new points carry polyline topology, and a path that was closed comes back closed.
- [pathSegments](#pathsegments) — Emits ONE POINT PER SEGMENT of every polyline primitive, placed and oriented so that spawning a unit-sized asset on it draws the path as solid geometry.
- [pointScatterOnPath](#pointscatteronpath) — Scatters `count` points at UNIFORMLY RANDOM ARC POSITIONS along EVERY polyline primitive of the `path` input and emits them as a NEW point cloud lying exactly ON the path.
- [segmentCrossings](#segmentcrossings) — Emits ONE POINT PER CROSSING among the input's SEGMENTS — its 2-vertex primitives (whatever their primtype: connectPoints' edges, connectByIndex's bars, pathClip or pathResample output made of two-point pieces) — carrying the two primitive ids and the crossing's directions, as a plain point cloud (no topology).
- [splineSample](#splinesample) — Samples points along polyline primitives by arc length, treating all polylines of the input as one concatenated curve.
- [surfaceSample](#surfacesample) — Scatters points on a triangle mesh: each of `count` candidates picks a triangle with probability proportional to its area, then a uniform position on it (uniform barycentric placement).
- [volumeSample](#volumesample) — Fills an axis-aligned box with a regular grid of points: each axis is divided into floor(extent / cellSize) cells (at least 1) and a point is placed at each cell center, then jittered inside its cell.

**source**

- [meshFromField](#meshfromfield) — Turns a scalar FIELD into a triangle mesh: the surface where the field crosses `isoValue`.
- [meshPrimitive](#meshprimitive) — Builds a parametric triangle mesh with no input: shape 'plane' is one axis-aligned rectangle, shape 'box' is six of them around a volume, shape 'cylinder' is an upright (y-axis) cylinder with optional caps, and shape 'disc' is one flat polygonal disc lying like a plane.
- [pointGrid](#pointgrid) — Creates a regular grid of points: countX * countY * countZ points starting at origin, stepped by spacing per axis.
- [pointLine](#pointline) — Creates `count` evenly spaced points on a straight line.
- [pointList](#pointlist) — Creates one point per [x, y, z] triple in `positions`, in list order, and writes named per-point columns from literal values: a TABLE of points carried in the graph file itself.
- [pointScatterInBounds](#pointscatterinbounds) — Scatters `count` points uniformly inside the axis-aligned box [boundsMin, boundsMax].
- [pointScatterInWorld](#pointscatterinworld) — Scatters points over an INFINITE lattice anchored to world coordinates, then returns the ones inside the query window [boundsMin, boundsMax).

**spawn**

- [spawnInstances](#spawninstances) — Spawner terminal: converts the input point cloud into render-agnostic instance batches.

**surface**

- [bridgePaths](#bridgepaths) — Joins each polyline on `from` to one polyline on `to` with a strip of quads, point i to point i: segment i of the one path, segment i of the other and the two rungs between their ends make one quad, emitted as two 3-vertex 'poly' triangles (the mesh convention every consumer here reads — surfaceSample, the uv and raycast transfers).
- [extrudePolygon](#extrudepolygon) — Treats every CLOSED polyline primitive as a polygon boundary and sweeps it along a direction into 3-vertex 'poly' triangles: a footprint becomes massing, a boundary loop becomes a wall.
- [polygonBoolean](#polygonboolean) — Planar boolean of closed polylines: the UNION, INTERSECTION or DIFFERENCE of the polygons on `in` (the subject) and the polygons on `clip`, computed in a plane (`plane`, 'xz' by default).
- [smoothNormals](#smoothnormals) — Rewrites the point `normal` (f32 tuple 3) of a triangle mesh so a faceted curve shades as the surface it approximates while a real edge stays sharp.
- [splitPolygons](#splitpolygons) — Cuts every polygon on `in` along the polylines on `cutters`, removing a strip `kerf` wide around each cut, and puts out the PIECES: the glass lights between a window's mullion bars, the panels of a facade between its joints, a slab cut into bays.
- [sweepProfile](#sweepprofile) — Places a cross-section on EVERY POINT of every polyline primitive and stitches consecutive placements into 3-vertex 'poly' triangles: a curve becomes a surface.

**value**

- [valueConstant](#valueconstant) — Emits a single constant number as a value item, for feeding value pins or tagging pipelines with plain data.

## applyRules

Composite node that runs a REWRITING GRAMMAR over labelled cells, one ROUND at a time: the recursion of a split grammar (a facade into floors, floors into panels, panels halved until they are small), in a graph that cannot wire a cycle. ONE INLINE BODY, AND A RULE IS A PIN PAIR: an exposed input and an exposed output with the SAME name form a rule, and that name is the LABEL it rewrites. Each round the frontier is partitioned by the string point attribute `labelAttr` ("piece", splitBox's): input X receives every cell labelled X, and output X's cells are that rule's products, whatever labels they carry, so a `panel` rule whose products are labelled `panel` again is a recursion. A label with no rule is TERMINAL: its cells leave on `out`, in the order they settle. An exposed input with NO same-named output is a BROADCAST, fed whole to every round (an occluder, a lookup cloud). So the node's own pins are `in` (the axiom cells, round 0's frontier) plus the broadcasts, and `out`, `trail` (the derivation trail, below), `rounds` (how many times the body cooked) and `converged` (1, or 0 when maxDepth cut the grammar off); the rule pairs are interior and are never wired. ONLY FED RULES COOK: each round cooks the body once, requesting only the outputs of rules that received cells, so a branch is never cooked on an empty input and may freely read a column only its own producers write. That is why the body is checked when it is built: output X may not depend on another rule's input (duplicate the node, or move it upstream of a broadcast; a node reading only broadcasts may be shared), input X must reach output X, a broadcast must reach some rule, and an output with no same-named input is refused (there are no side outputs). VARIANTS AND STOPPING ARE RELABELS IN THE BRANCH, never wrapper params: a rule picks a variant with setAttribute type "string", values ["square", "arched"] and an index field reading the roll, lt(attribute("ruleRoll"), 0.4); it stops a recursion by relabelling small cells to a terminal label (values ["panel", "leaf"], index lt(max(box("sx"), box("sy")), 1.2)) and cutting the rest with a splitBox whose `where` is attributeIs("piece", "panel"). EVERY RELABEL COSTS ONE ROUND of depth, so size maxDepth for it. ITERATION METADATA: every cell gets `depthAttr` (i32) as it is classified, the round, which is its lineage depth; every FED cell also gets `rollAttr` (f32 in [0, 1)), hashed from this node's seed, the rule's label and a SCOPE identity: the cell's own by default (and the round, so a relabel-only recursion draws afresh each round), or, with `scoped`/`scopeOf`, its nearest ancestor that was fed to the named rule ("root" for its axiom cell), so every window under one facade rolls the same number. A scoped cell with no such ancestor is refused, and a rule scoped to itself is refused as a no-op. PRODUCTS MUST DERIVE FROM THE RULE'S INPUT: each fed cell carries a u32 lineage column `__ruleSrc`, and a product that lost it or names a cell fed to another rule (a fresh pointList merged in, a broadcast cell) is refused by name. On `out` the column is handed back as the axiom cell carried it on `in` (removed when `in` had none), so an applyRules nests inside another's rule branch, directly or inside a subgraph. A column one rule writes and another does not is default-filled on the other's cells when a round's products are concatenated (mergePoints' union, in the body's exposed-output order). TERMINATION IS REPORTED, NOT THROWN: cells still feeding a rule at maxDepth go to `out` unexpanded, `converged` is 0, and the detail attribute `rulesCutOff` on `out` (i32, one component per rule in the body's exposed-output order, always written, all zero when converged) counts them per rule, with `rulesCutOffRules` (a string tuple, same order) naming the rule behind each component; a grammar grown past 4194304 cells is refused, naming the round. THE DERIVATION TRAIL: `trail` holds every cell that was FED to a rule, one row per rule application, as it was fed (its own columns and box, its label, which is the rule it was fed to, its `depthAttr` and `rollAttr`). Rows run round by round, and within a round rule by rule in the body's exposed-output order, each rule's cells in frontier order. `parentAttr` (i32, default "ruleParent") is the parent link on `trail` AND on `out`: the `trail` ROW of the rule application that produced the cell, or -1 for a cell that came in on `in`; so on `trail` a row's ruleParent is always smaller than its own index, and following it walks a cell's ancestors back to its axiom. Read an ancestor's box with transferByIndex (source the trail, index attribute("ruleParent"), outOfRange "miss" for the roots), or draw the derivation tree with connectByIndex over the trail (rootValue -1); filter the trail by label to get, say, every storey a later stage must not cover. A cell cut off at maxDepth was never fed, so it is on `out` and not on `trail`. The row numbering is a fact of the grammar, not of the wiring, so `out`'s links are the same whether or not `trail` is wired, and the trail is BUILT ONLY WHEN SOMETHING READS IT (a connection or a declared graph output): unread, it is an empty collection and the loop retains no cell. A nested applyRules' trail is its own; the outer node rewrites the parent column its products carry. Wiring the trail never fails a cook that succeeds unwired: a column the trail's rows (cells of different rounds) carry in two shapes is left off the trail and named in its detail attribute `trailDroppedColumns`, and a column named `parentAttr` in another shape is refused where it appears, under either wiring. A grammar whose trail would pass 4194304 rows is refused, naming the round, whether or not `trail` is wired, so the links on `out` always name rows that exist. SEEDS: the body cooks under one seed for every round, hashCombine(nodeSeed, hashString("applyRules")); splitBox re-seeds each piece from its parent, so a cell's draws follow its lineage rather than the round. That holds only for rules that RE-SEED: a relabel keeps the cell's identity, so a randomField in a relabel-only recursion draws the parent's number again every round (it stops at once or never); a per-round chance reads `ruleRoll` instead. A cell's subtree is independent of its siblings only when every node in its branches is ELEMENTWISE: an attributeReduce or neighbourhood node couples all cells of one label in one round, and `out` indices shift whenever a sibling's output changes. The loop is CPU; a branch's field params may resolve on the GPU device, each node recording its own fallback. Inline payload only: no `ref` in this version. Create instances with applyRulesNode(innerGraph, exposedInputs, exposedOutputs, exposedParams), or deserialize a graph containing one.

**Category:** composite

**Meters the cook budget itself:** under `cook(graph, { budgetMs })` this node yields to the event loop inside its own execute, so its `NodeDoneInfo.elapsedMs` is wall time spanning those yields — not an uninterrupted block. Derive a longest-block figure from a cook with the budget unset.

**Inputs:** *(none)*

**Outputs:** *(none)*

**Examples:** [basics-apply-rules.json](../graphs/basics-apply-rules.json), [examples-brick-house.json](../graphs/examples-brick-house.json) — 2 total (`pcg graphs --node applyRules`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `maxDepth` | u32 | `32` | 1..1024 |  |  | The most ROUNDS the grammar runs: the body cooks at most this many times, and a cell is expanded at lineage depth maxDepth - 1 at the deepest. EVERY RELABEL COSTS A ROUND — a rule that only picks a variant (window -> arched \| square) or marks a cell finished (panel -> leaf) is one round of depth for the cells it relabels — so count the relabels when sizing it: a facade split into floors, floors into panels, panels halved until small and then given a styled window is easily fifteen rounds deep. Reaching the cap is NOT an error and is not silent: the cells still carrying a rule's label go to "out" unexpanded, "converged" reports 0 and the detail attribute "rulesCutOff" on "out" counts them per rule, with "rulesCutOffRules" naming the rule behind each component, so the rule that would not stop is named. A grammar that GROWS every round is stopped sooner, by the 4194304-point cap, which is refused naming the round and the rule. |
| `labelAttr` | string | `"piece"` |  |  |  | The STRING point attribute (tuple 1) the frontier is dispatched on: a cell whose label equals a rule's name is fed to that rule, any other label is terminal and goes to "out". splitBox's labelAttr default, so a split's labels ARE the rules' names. Required on "in" and on every rule's products; a missing or non-string column is refused naming the rule that dropped it. |
| `depthAttr` | string | `"ruleDepth"` |  |  |  | The i32 point attribute (tuple 1) written on every cell as it is classified: the ROUND, which is the cell's lineage depth (an "in" cell is 0, its products 1, ...). A rule reads it as iteration metadata — lt(attribute("ruleDepth"), 3) caps a recursion at a fixed depth. On "out" it is the depth the cell settled at (maxDepth on a cell the cap cut off). Empty writes none. Not a standard attribute, and not the same name as labelAttr or rollAttr. |
| `rollAttr` | string | `"ruleRoll"` |  |  |  | The f32 point attribute (tuple 1) written on every FED cell: a number in [0, 1) hashed from this node's seed, the rule's label and the cell's SCOPE identity (see "scoped"); an UNSCOPED roll also folds in the round, so a rule that keeps a cell's label and identity (a relabel-only recursion: "stay a panel while the roll is under 0.7") draws afresh every round. The branch picks a variant with it — setAttribute string, values ["arched", "square"], value lt(attribute("ruleRoll"), 0.5) — so the weights sit in the branch, resolved on that rule's cells only. Different rules roll independently even under one scope. On "out" a cell carries the roll of the rule application that produced it (0 on a cell that arrived terminal). Empty writes none, and is refused when "scoped" lists anything, since the scope would then reach no column. |
| `scoped` | stringList | `[]` |  |  |  | Rules whose ROLL is SHARED by a scope, parallel to "scopeOf": rule scoped[i] rolls on the identity of its NEAREST ANCESTOR fed to rule scopeOf[i], so every such cell under one ancestor rolls the same number — one window style per facade is scoped ["window"], scopeOf ["facade"]. A rule not listed rolls on its own cell's identity, so every cell differs. The roll is shared; the DECISION is shared only if the branch's selector reads nothing per cell besides the roll. A RECURSIVE rule scoped to an ancestor rolls the same number at every depth and so decides the same at every depth, which is usually a mistake. Each entry must name a rule (did-you-mean otherwise), once. |
| `scopeOf` | stringList | `[]` |  |  |  | The scope of each "scoped" entry, same length: the name of a RULE (the nearest ancestor that was fed to it), or "root" — the cell on "in" the scoped cell descends from, which every cell has. A rule scoped to ITSELF is refused: its nearest ancestor-or-self fed to it is the cell itself, which is exactly what not listing it does. A cell with NO ancestor fed to its scope rule — it arrived on "in" already carrying the scoped label, or came down a branch that never passed through the scope rule — is REFUSED, naming the rule, the round and the scope, rather than rolled on some other identity: scope it to "root", or route every such cell through the scope rule. |
| `parentAttr` | string | `"ruleParent"` |  |  |  | The i32 point attribute (tuple 1) linking a cell to the rule application it came from, written on "out" and on "trail": the ROW of "trail" holding the cell that a rule rewrote into this one, or -1 for a cell that came in on "in" (an axiom cell, or a terminal one that never fed a rule). "trail" lists every FED cell round by round, and within a round rule by rule in the body's exposed-output order, each rule's cells in frontier order — so on "trail" a row's ruleParent is always smaller than the row's own index, and following it from any cell walks its ancestors back to the axiom. Read an ancestor's box with transferByIndex (source "trail", index attribute("ruleParent"), outOfRange "miss" for the -1 roots), or draw the derivation tree with connectByIndex over "trail" (rootValue -1). The numbering does not depend on whether "trail" is wired, so the links on "out" always mean the same rows. Empty writes none. Not a standard attribute, and not the same name as labelAttr, depthAttr or rollAttr. A column of this name already on the cells in another shape is refused where it appears (on "in", or on the output of the rule that wrote it), the same whether or not "trail" is wired. |

## arcTile

Lays a REPEATED PIECE over arc ranges of a path: one oriented instance point every `spacing` units between each range's start and end, ready for spawnInstances. This is the tile-a-tunnel-out-of-one-rib operation, and it is a primitive rather than a convenience because ENCLOSURE IS A PATTERN, NOT AN ASSET — a heavily enclosed lap needs its cover held up by scores of separate objects, no single one of them much of the total, the workhorse being one strip placed dozens of times. There is no tunnel model to find and place; there is a run of repeated pieces over an arc range, which is what this builds. THE RANGES ARRIVE AS A SECOND GEOMETRY, one point per range, carrying the range's start and length as point attributes (`runStart` and `runSpan` by default, which are runFit's own defaults, so a detected run re-tiles without renaming anything). A point cloud rather than params because there are MANY ranges and each carries its own decisions — where it starts, how long it is, which piece it is made of, how wide, which variant — and a param is one value for the cook. It also makes every existing node a range producer: scatter them, filter them, snap them, reject the ones that overlap, all before a single tile exists. ATOMICITY IS THE WHOLE POINT AND IT IS STRUCTURAL. A tunnel is the SAME piece repeated; drawing a fresh piece per rib reopens the seams the overlap was there to close, and in the case this node comes from a planned 17-unit covered stretch measured back as 8 the moment poses were drawn per piece. So the choice is made ONCE, upstream, on the range point — where there is exactly one element per run to make it on — and `rangeNames` COPIES it onto every tile of that range. Copying is what makes it atomic: a per-tile draw can be uniform only by luck, and only until someone changes the seed. The tile's own `seed` is rooted in the range for a weaker but related reason — it is hashCombine(the node's seed, hashCombine(the range's own seed, the range's index), the tile's index within the range), so the tiles of one range keep their per-tile jitter when a DIFFERENT range changes its spacing, which a seed taken from the global tile index cannot offer. The range's INDEX is folded in as well as its seed because a ranges cloud that never had a seed written carries a column of zeros, and rooting on that alone would hand every range identical tiles. SPACING IS A CEILING ON THE PITCH, NOT THE PITCH. A range takes ceil(length / spacing) tiles, placed at the centres of that many EQUAL sub-intervals, so the actual step is length / count and is at most `spacing`. Rounded UP so pieces at least abut: rounding to nearest leaves the pitch wider than asked whenever a range is not a whole number of pieces long, and a gap in a tiled cover is not a near-miss but a hole — a planned 15-unit run was closing 9.6, under the 10 that counted as a long stretch at all. Nothing here knows how big your piece is: to OVERLAP, give a spacing smaller than the piece. THE MOUTHS FLARE. `flare` is the arc distance over which each end opens and `taper` the scale the very mouth reaches, applied to the two `scale` components that are not the `axis` — so the cross-section opens and the length along the path does not, which is what makes a driver see an opening rather than a wall and what keeps the entry clear at the moment it matters. `flareAttr` writes the raw 0..1 ramp instead, for everything else a mouth might do (lift, tilt, a different asset), because WHAT flares is the asset's business and not this node's. ON A CLOSED PATH RANGES WRAP: a range crossing the seam is one range, its arc taken modulo the path's length, matching what pathRuns and runFit do with a run there. On an OPEN path a range reaching past either end is REFUSED rather than clamped — a clamped range is a shorter tunnel than the one that was planned, reported as success. THE OUTPUT IS A PLAIN CLOUD, not a path: the tiles are placements along the curve, not the curve, and no polyline topology is built over them, exactly as pathSegments leaves its segment midpoints. Each tile carries P, `rot` (the `axis` turned onto the path's tangent, roll fixed by an up hint of [0, 1, 0] with orientAlongVector's deterministic fallbacks), `scale` (1, tapered at the mouths), `tangent`, `curveU`, `seed`, the standard point attributes, whatever `rangeNames` carries, and the PATH's primitive attributes — so a road's width reaches the ribs standing over it. It emits no randomness of its own.

**Category:** sampler

**Inputs:** `path` (geometry), `ranges` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-tile-an-arc.json](../graphs/basics-tile-an-arc.json), [basics-colonnade.json](../graphs/basics-colonnade.json) — 2 total (`pcg graphs --node arcTile`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `startAttr` | string | `"runStart"` |  |  |  | POINT attribute on the RANGES input (tuple 1, numeric) holding where each range begins, as an arc length in WORLD UNITS along the path — the same coordinate pathResample steps and pathPointAt's 'distance' mode reads. Defaults to runFit's `startAttr` suggestion so a detected run tiles without renaming; note the one conversion that pairing needs, which is that runFit may have been run against a CUSTOM arc coordinate in other units (a lap measured in half-widths), and this node reads world units. On a CLOSED path the value wraps: any real number is legal and is taken modulo the path's length. On an OPEN path a range must lie inside the path, and one that does not is refused rather than clamped. |
| `lengthAttr` | string | `"runSpan"` |  |  |  | POINT attribute on the RANGES input (tuple 1, numeric) holding how much arc each range covers, in world units. Must be > 0 on every range: a range with no length is a single placement rather than a tiling, and this node refuses one instead of emitting a lone tile that looks like a very short tunnel — drop it upstream (filterByAttribute on the length) or place it with pathPointAt. On a closed path it may not exceed the path's own length, since the tiles would lap the loop and cover the same arc twice. Must differ from `startAttr`: a start and a length are two values, and one column cannot hold both. |
| `pathAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | POINT attribute on the RANGES input (tuple 1, numeric) naming WHICH polyline each range belongs to, as an index into the path input's PRIMITIVE domain — the same numbering every error message here uses. Empty (the default) is legal only when the path input holds exactly ONE polyline, which is the ordinary case; with several, this is REQUIRED rather than defaulted to the first, because tiling the wrong road is a cook that looks entirely fine. An index that is not a polyline of the input is refused, listing the ones that are. |
| `spacing` | f32 | `1` | >= 0 |  | yes | Ceiling on the distance between consecutive tiles, in world units. A range of length L takes max(1, ceil(L / spacing)) tiles at the centres of that many equal sub-intervals, so the PITCH is L / count and is at most this value — never more. Rounded up rather than to nearest so that pieces meant to abut do: a nearest-rounded pitch is wider than asked whenever the range is not a whole number of pieces long, and a gap in tiled cover is a hole rather than a near-miss. This node does not know how big your piece is, so overlap is spelled as a spacing SMALLER than the piece — about 5% under is enough to close the wedge two pieces leave on the outside of a bend. Must be > 0. AS A FIELD IT IS ONE SPACING PER RANGE, resolved on the RANGES input's POINT domain, which is what lets the pitch follow the piece: a range that chose a 5-unit strip and one that chose a 1-unit rib tile at their own pitches in ONE cook, reading the size the range itself carries. A field is not range-checked the way a plain value is, so bound the expression with max(<expr>, <the smallest pitch you meant>); a NaN or infinite spacing is refused, naming this param, and so is the whole cook when the resolved spacings would place more than 1048576 tiles. |
| `flare` | f32 | `0` | >= 0 |  | yes | Arc distance over which each MOUTH of a range opens, in world units; 0 (the default) is no flare. Every tile gets a ramp of 1 at the very mouth falling to 0 at this distance inside, taken from whichever end is nearer, and the ramp drives `taper` and `flareAttr`. It exists because a tiled cover that starts at full section is a wall with a hole in it: the eye reads an opening from the way the section grows, and the flare is also what keeps the view clear at the moment of entry, which is the moment it matters. A flare of at least half the range's length ramps every tile (the two mouths meet in the middle) and is legal — the ramps do not add, each tile taking the nearer mouth's. One value per RANGE as a field, resolved on the ranges' POINT domain exactly as `spacing` is, so a long tunnel and a short gantry can open over different distances in one cook. |
| `taper` | f32 | `1` | >= 0 |  | yes | The `scale` a tile reaches AT the mouth, interpolated to 1 at `flare` inside; 1 (the default) is no taper, and with `flare` at 0 this is read nowhere. Applied to the two scale components that are NOT `axis` — the cross-section — so the mouth opens or pinches while the piece's length along the path is left alone; scaling all three would make the mouth pieces longer as well as wider and leave gaps between them. Above 1 opens the mouth (1.5 is a mouth half again as tall and wide), below 1 pinches it, which is what a run that should taper away to nothing at its ends wants. One value per RANGE as a field, on the ranges' POINT domain. |
| `axis` | enum | `"+z"` |  | `+x`, `-x`, `+y`, `-y`, `+z`, `-z` |  | Which local axis of the tiled asset points ALONG the path, and therefore which two `scale` components `taper` opens. Default '+z', matching orientAlongVector rather than pathSegments' '+y': this node points a piece at a heading and does not stretch it to span anything, where pathSegments puts the SEGMENT'S LENGTH on the chosen axis to draw a tube. Roll around the path is fixed by an up hint of [0, 1, 0] with the same deterministic fallbacks orientAlongVector uses ([0, 0, 1], then [1, 0, 0]) — never random, and never left to whatever a degenerate cross product produced. |
| `flareAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | OPT-IN: POINT attribute (f32) on the output receiving each tile's raw FLARE RAMP — 1 at the mouth, falling linearly to 0 at `flare` inside, and 0 everywhere on a range with no flare. Empty (the default) writes none and the output is byte-identical to a cook without it. It is here because WHAT a mouth does is the asset's business and not this node's: `taper` covers the common case of opening the section, and everything else — lifting the roof, tilting the pieces outward, swapping in a wider variant at the entrance, fading a material — is a field or a setAttribute reading this column. The ramp is per tile, taken from the NEARER mouth, so a range shorter than twice its flare has every tile ramped and none of them at zero. |
| `tileIndexAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | OPT-IN: POINT attribute (i32) receiving each tile's 0-BASED INDEX WITHIN ITS OWN RANGE, restarting at 0 for every range. Empty (the default) writes none. It is the per-range coordinate nothing else can state: this node emits NEW points, so an index written upstream with setAttribute does not survive, and `curveU` is a fraction of the whole path rather than of the range. Without it, 'every other rib' has to be spelled on the GLOBAL point index, which agrees with the per-range one only while every range emits the same, EVEN, number of tiles and reports nothing when that stops being true. Index 0 is the tile nearest the range's start in the path's walk direction, including on a range that wraps a closed path's seam. |
| `rangeIndexAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | OPT-IN: POINT attribute (i32) receiving the index of the RANGE a tile came from, in the ranges input's own point order. Empty (the default) writes none. This is the group key: partitionByAttribute splits one item per run, pointsToPath's `groupAttr` threads each run's tiles into a path of their own, and attributeReduce collapses per run — 'how long did this run actually come out' is a reduction over this. It also names the atomic unit for anything that must be decided once per run and is easier to write downstream than upstream. |
| `rangeNames` | stringList | `[]` |  |  |  | Point attributes of the RANGES input to copy onto every tile of that range, in any order; empty (the default) carries nothing, which is not an error. THIS IS THE ATOMICITY MECHANISM. A tunnel is one piece repeated, so the piece is chosen ONCE — on the range point, where there is exactly one element per run to choose on, by whatever upstream node makes that kind of decision — and copied here, unchanged, onto every tile. A per-tile draw is uniform only by accident: the case this node comes from measured a planned 17-unit covered stretch back as 8 the moment poses were drawn per piece, because varying the shape along a run reopens the seams the overlap existed to close. Carry the asset id, the variant, the yaw, the width, the material — anything that must be the same all the way along. Each column arrives with the range's own type, tuple size and default. Three names are refused rather than resolved silently: one this node writes itself (P, rot, scale, density, boundsMin, boundsMax, color, seed, tangent, curveU, and whatever flareAttr/tileIndexAttr/rangeIndexAttr named) — copy it to another name on the ranges first with setAttribute and carry that; a name repeated in the list; and a name the ranges input does not have, which is an error listing what it does have. |

## attributeReduce

Collapses a numeric attribute over a whole domain into a single value on the detail domain: sum, min, max, average (all f32, componentwise, keeping the source tuple size) or count (u32, the number of elements — this mode reads no attribute). NaN elements are ignored by every mode, and average divides by however many were left, so one bad element cannot destroy the statistic; this is the deliberate difference from promoteAttribute point->detail, which propagates NaN. The other difference is outName: two reductions of one attribute (a min AND a max) can coexist, which promoting cannot do because both would land on the same detail name. Over an empty domain sum and average are 0, min is Infinity, max is -Infinity, count is 0. Note that a detail attribute is NOT readable from a point-domain field (a field reads the domain it lands on), so a reduction is for hosts, inspection and cook-stat reporting — to rescale an attribute by its own observed range use attributeRemap's 'fit' mode, which measures it internally.

**Category:** attribute

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-repeat-until-settled.json](../graphs/basics-repeat-until-settled.json), [basics-report-to-the-host.json](../graphs/basics-report-to-the-host.json), [examples-coral.json](../graphs/examples-coral.json) — 6 total (`pcg graphs --node attributeReduce`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `name` | string | `"density"` |  |  |  | Numeric attribute to reduce (tuple 1..4). Must exist on `domain`. Ignored by mode 'count', which counts elements and may leave this empty. |
| `domain` | enum | `"point"` |  | `point`, `vertex`, `primitive`, `detail` |  | Domain to reduce over: point, vertex, primitive, or detail (one element). |
| `mode` | enum | `"max"` |  | `sum`, `min`, `max`, `average`, `count` |  | How the elements collapse: sum, min, max, average (each componentwise over the tuple, NaN elements skipped), or count (how many elements the domain has, ignoring `name`). |
| `outName` | string | `""` |  |  |  | Name of the detail attribute to write. Empty (the default) reuses `name`, which is what promoting would have produced; give distinct names to hold several reductions of one attribute at once. The shape is this node's to pick (f32 at the source's tuple size, or u32 for mode 'count'), so a name the DETAIL domain already holds under a different shape is REFUSED rather than deleted and re-added — writing it would destroy that column while the cook still looked fine. A same-shape column is reused and reset. The one exception is reducing a detail attribute into its own name: there the column replaced is the column read, so it converts in place. |

## attributeRemap

Rescales a numeric attribute linearly from an input range to an output range, writing f32. Mode 'range' uses inMin/inMax as given — the hand-tuned remap(x, -1, 1, 0, 1) that every noise-driven graph writes. Mode 'fit' MEASURES the attribute's own range over the domain first (ignoring NaN) and uses that, which is what turns any invented quantity — a neighbor count, a hand-built score — into a usable 0..1 density or color input without knowing its scale in advance; it is also why this node needs no help from attributeReduce, whose detail-domain output no field or param could have read back anyway. An empty input range (inMin == inMax, or a fit over zero usable elements) maps everything to outMin, matching the `remap` field function. Tuples remap componentwise against one shared range, and NaN stays NaN in every mode — including the empty-range case, so unmeasurable data never turns into a valid-looking value. Reversed output ranges are fine and invert the values. All four window ends accept a FIELD, which makes the range PER ELEMENT rather than shared: each element is rescaled against its own window, the tuple still sharing that element's one range, and every rule above (empty range maps to outMin, NaN stays NaN, reversed inverts) then holds element by element instead of domain-wide. Mode 'fit' is the exception it already was — it measures inMin/inMax and never reads them, field or not.

**Category:** attribute

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-attribute-remap.json](../graphs/basics-attribute-remap.json) — 1 total (`pcg graphs --node attributeRemap`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `name` | string | `"density"` |  |  |  | Numeric attribute to rescale (tuple 1..4). Must exist on `domain`. |
| `outName` | string | `""` |  |  |  | Name of the f32 attribute to write on the same domain. Empty (the default) rewrites `name` in place, which also converts an integer attribute to f32. Writing over a DIFFERENT attribute that already exists with another shape is REFUSED rather than deleted and re-added — remapping into an i32 `id` or into `P` would destroy that column while the cook still looked fine. A same-shape column is reused, and the in-place conversion above stays legal because there the column replaced is the one being read. |
| `domain` | enum | `"point"` |  | `point`, `vertex`, `primitive`, `detail` |  | Domain the attribute lives on: point, vertex, primitive, or detail. |
| `mode` | enum | `"range"` |  | `range`, `fit` |  | 'range' takes the input range from inMin/inMax; 'fit' measures the attribute's actual minimum and maximum over the domain and ignores inMin/inMax — a FIELD in either of them is ignored the same way, and is not evaluated at all. Only the INPUT window is measured: outMin/outMax are read in both modes, fields included. |
| `inMin` | f32 | `-1` |  |  | yes | Value mapped to outMin, in mode 'range'. As a FIELD it is a PER-ELEMENT window end, evaluated on `domain`, so each element is rescaled against its OWN input range instead of one range shared by the whole domain: a per-point noise floor, or a threshold read from another attribute, turns the same source values into different results for different elements — which no single number can do. The window is per ELEMENT, not per component: a tuple attribute remaps all of its components against that element's one window, as it already does against the shared one. Mode 'fit' IGNORES this param and does not evaluate it at all — a field there is dead exactly as a plain number is, because fit measures the attribute's own minimum and maximum instead. A degenerate window is a per-element outcome, not a refusal: where inMin resolves equal to inMax that element maps to outMin (NaN sources stay NaN), the same rule the shared empty range follows. Non-finite is refused, naming this param, since an infinite span maps everything to 0 and a NaN one erases the attribute. |
| `inMax` | f32 | `1` |  |  | yes | Value mapped to outMax, in mode 'range'. As a FIELD it is a PER-ELEMENT window end evaluated on `domain`, the upper counterpart of inMin: the two together give each element its own input range, so 'what counts as high here' can vary across the domain — the per-element version of the hand-tuned remap(x, -1, 1, 0, 1). Reversed per element is fine (inMin above inMax) and inverts that element only. Mode 'fit' IGNORES this param and does not evaluate it at all. Where it resolves equal to inMin the window is empty and that element maps to outMin, NaN sources excepted (they stay NaN). Non-finite is refused, naming this param. |
| `outMin` | f32 | `0` |  |  | yes | Value inMin maps to. As a FIELD it is a PER-ELEMENT output floor, evaluated on `domain`, so each element lands in its own destination range — the way to remap a shared quantity into per-element bands (a scale whose floor follows the point's size, a colour ramp whose ends differ by region) in one node. Read per element in every mode, INCLUDING 'fit', which measures only the input window; and it is also the value an empty window (inMin == inMax, or a fit over nothing usable) maps that element to, so a fielded outMin decides the degenerate case too. With clamp on, the bounds are this element's own outMin/outMax, whichever is smaller and larger. Non-finite is refused, naming this param. |
| `outMax` | f32 | `1` |  |  | yes | Value inMax maps to. As a FIELD it is a PER-ELEMENT output ceiling, evaluated on `domain`, the upper counterpart of outMin: with both fielded, every element gets its own output band, and reversing them per element (outMax below outMin) inverts that element alone. Read per element in every mode, INCLUDING 'fit'. Note that an empty input window maps to outMin, not to this param, so a per-element ceiling has no effect where the window collapsed. Non-finite is refused, naming this param. |
| `clamp` | bool | `false` |  |  |  | Hold results inside the output range (whichever of outMin/outMax is smaller or larger). False (the default) extrapolates, matching the `remap` field function; true is the usual choice when the result feeds density or color. In mode 'fit' it only affects NaN-free data trivially, since fitted values already land inside the range. |

## boxContact

Asks, for every BOX on the `in` pin (the QUERIES), whether it meets any box on the `targets` pin, and writes the answer onto the query points as attributes: `hitAttr` a bool (any target met), `countAttr` an i32 (how many distinct targets), `targetAttr` an i32 (the index of the met target whose centre is nearest the centre of the query box as tested, -1 for none). Nothing else changes — same points, same order, same topology, every attribute carried — so the usual next step is a filter or a relabel on the flag. This is the CONTEXT QUERY of a split grammar (the occlusion query of the shape-grammar literature, and the box form of a point-in-volume or group-by-bounding-object test): is this window blocked by a neighbour or a chimney, does this bay cross any box of another set, is this room inside the lot. A BOX is a point's standard `boundsMin`/`boundsMax`, read in one of two FRAMES, chosen per input by `queryFrame` and `targetFrame`: "world" (the default) reads the two corners as WORLD coordinates and does not read P, rot or scale — the convention setBounds, splitBox and packBricks write, so grammar cells need nothing done to them; "local" reads them in the point's OWN frame, mapped through P, rot and scale exactly as spawnInstances places a mesh (world = P + rot * (scale * corner)), so a rotated box is tested as the ORIENTED box it is (rot is normalized first, so a non-unit quaternion places the box rigidly where spawnInstances' matrix would also scale it). THE MODE (`mode`) is the question: "overlap" — the two boxes CROSS: on every separating direction their extents overlap by more than zero, so boxes that only share a face, an edge or a corner do NOT overlap (two neighbouring grammar cells are not overlapping), while a zero-thickness box passing through a solid one does; "touch" — the same test closed, so sharing a face, edge or corner COUNTS (the party-wall neighbour); "contain" — the target lies wholly inside the query, boundary included; "inside" — the query lies wholly inside the target, boundary included. THE TESTS ARE EXACT for oriented boxes too — the separating-axis test on all fifteen axes for overlap and touch, the support extent along the outer box's three axes for contain and inside — with two stated approximations that apply only where a box is ROTATED: a cross axis of two edges within 1e-6 rad of parallel is skipped as rounding noise, which can only turn "apart" into "meet", across a gap of at most about 1e-6 of the boxes' size; and a gap or an overlap within 1e-12 of the pair's scale (its largest centre coordinate plus both boxes' half-extents), on any axis, is read as EXACT contact in both directions — "touch" accepts it and "overlap" rejects it — so two boxes under one P and rot that share a face share it under every rotation rather than as rounding decides. Axis-aligned pairs (every "world" box, and "local" ones with no rotation) are compared exactly. THE MARGINS move the QUERY box's faces before the test, per query point, in world units along the box's own three axes (scale is not applied to them), and leave the point's own bounds untouched. `margin` GROWS BOTH faces of each axis: margin 0.5 with "touch" asks "is any target within half a unit of this box, measured per axis" — a box-shaped neighbourhood, not a rounded one; a vec3 gives each axis its own reach, and a scalar field broadcasts to all three. `marginMin` and `marginMax` move ONE face each — the face on the boundsMin side and the face on the boundsMax side — signed, so they reach out of one face only (a wall whose outside is -z: marginMin vec(0, 0, 2) looks two units out of the front and not into the house behind it), shift the box (marginMin -d with marginMax +d moves it d), or carve the query down to a probe of its own: the window test is the bay's box with its outward face pushed half a stud out, its inward face pulled to meet it, and its sides and height pulled in to the opening, all as fields reading the bay's box names and attributes. The three add: the min face moves out by margin + marginMin, the max face by margin + marginMax. A NEGATIVE total shrinks the query, which is how a containment test tolerates a sliver ("inside" with margin -0.01), and a query shrunk past nothing on any axis is EMPTY and meets nothing in every mode. EXCLUSION: `excludeAttr` names a tuple-1 attribute present on BOTH inputs, and a target whose value EQUALS the query's is skipped — the bay that must not count its own wall carries its wall's id (write one with setAttribute before the split; splitBox carries every attribute onto its pieces), and siblings or one parent are excluded the same way. Numbers compare by value whatever their numeric type (NaN never equals anything); strings compare by text, so the two inputs' string tables may differ. `excludeSelf` skips target i for query i, for one cloud wired to BOTH pins ("does this box overlap any OTHER box of its set"); it is refused when the two inputs have different point counts, since they cannot then be one cloud. STAGING: query against an EARLIER stage — another branch's finished output, or an applyRules `trail` (the consumed ancestors, whose boxes are the blocks and storeys a later stage needs) — never against the frontier still being rewritten. DETERMINISM: nothing is drawn, nothing is accumulated, and the nearest target is the LOWEST INDEX among the hits whose squared centre distance ties with the least, within a band scaled like the rounding a centre carries (1e-12 of the least squared distance plus 2e-12 of it times the query centre's largest coordinate), so the answers do not depend on the order the targets arrive in beyond that index, and a rotation's rounding cannot break a true tie. COST: near linear in the two counts for any mix of sizes — the targets are binned by centre into one uniform grid per SIZE CLASS (radii doubling), and each query reads each class's grid within its own half-diagonal plus that class's largest, a bounding-sphere bound (two boxes can meet only if their centres are within the sum of their half-diagonals) that changes the cost, never the answer. Under a partitioned cook it is exact given every target whose centre lies within the query's half-diagonal (margins included, measured from the moved centre) plus the largest target's half-diagonal. ERRORS name the pin and point: a box that is not finite or is inside out (min above max), a missing bounds pair, a frame of "local" on points without P. CPU ONLY, and deliberately not a GPU adopter: the margin is a value that decides a contact, and a device-rounded margin one ulp off flips a touching pair — the same reason filterByExpression keeps its predicate on the CPU.

**Category:** point op

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry), `targets` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [examples-brick-house.json](../graphs/examples-brick-house.json) — 1 total (`pcg graphs --node boxContact`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `mode` | enum | `"overlap"` |  | `overlap`, `touch`, `contain`, `inside` |  | The question asked of each (query, target) pair. "overlap": the boxes CROSS — sharing only a face, an edge or a corner is NOT an overlap, so neighbouring grammar cells that tile a block do not overlap each other. "touch": the closed test — they overlap OR share a face, edge or corner; with a margin, "within that distance along each of the query's axes". "contain": the target lies wholly inside the (grown) query, boundary included. "inside": the (grown) query lies wholly inside the target, boundary included. Decided once per cook. |
| `margin` | vec3 | `[0,0,0]` |  |  | yes | How far the QUERY box is GROWN on BOTH sides along each of its own three axes before the test, in world units [x, y, z] (scale is not applied; for a "world" query the axes are the world's). 0 tests the box as it is. As a FIELD it is resolved per QUERY point — a scalar broadcasts to all three axes — so each query can reach as far as it needs. vec(0, 0, 2) on a wall running along x grows both its faces, the inside one too; to reach out of ONE face, use marginMin or marginMax instead. NEGATIVE shrinks the query: "inside" with a small negative margin tolerates a sliver of overhang, and "overlap" with one ignores a contact shallower than it. Adds to marginMin and marginMax. A query whose size after all three is below zero on any axis is EMPTY and meets nothing, in every mode. Must be finite. |
| `marginMin` | vec3 | `[0,0,0]` |  |  | yes | How far the query box's MIN-SIDE face — the one its boundsMin corner is on, along each of its own axes — moves OUT before the test, in world units [x, y, z], on top of `margin`. Signed: positive reaches out of that face only, negative pulls it in, and with marginMax it can shift the box (marginMin -d, marginMax +d moves it d along the axis) or carve a probe out of it. A wall whose outside is -z looks two units out of its front, and not into the house, with marginMin vec(0, 0, 2). As a FIELD it is resolved per QUERY point and may read the query's box names: the window probe half a stud in front of a south wall, flattened onto the outside, is marginMin vec(0, 0, 0.5) with marginMax vec(0, 0, -0.5 - box("sz")), plus whatever pulls the sides in to the opening. For a "local" query the side is the box's own, before its scale's sign. Must be finite. |
| `marginMax` | vec3 | `[0,0,0]` |  |  | yes | How far the query box's MAX-SIDE face — the one its boundsMax corner is on, along each of its own axes — moves OUT before the test, in world units [x, y, z], on top of `margin`. Signed, with the same readings as marginMin, mirrored: a wall whose outside is +z reaches out of its front with marginMax vec(0, 0, 2). As a FIELD it is resolved per QUERY point. Must be finite. |
| `queryFrame` | enum | `"world"` |  | `world`, `local` |  | How the QUERY points' boundsMin/boundsMax are read. "world" (the default): as world coordinates, P, rot and scale not read — the convention of setBounds, splitBox and packBricks, and so of every grammar cell. "local": in the point's own frame, placed by P, rot and scale exactly as spawnInstances places a mesh (world = P + rot * (scale * corner)); a rotated point is then tested as an ORIENTED box, exactly. Decided once per cook. |
| `targetFrame` | enum | `"world"` |  | `world`, `local` |  | How the TARGET points' boundsMin/boundsMax are read, with the same two choices as `queryFrame`, chosen independently: grammar cells ("world") can be queried against placed, rotated instances ("local"). Decided once per cook. |
| `excludeAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | OPT-IN: the name of a tuple-1 point attribute present on BOTH inputs; a target whose value EQUALS the query's is skipped, as if absent. This is how a bay does not count its own wall (give the wall an id before splitting it — splitBox carries every attribute onto its pieces — and give the targets the same column), and how siblings or the children of one parent ignore each other. Numbers compare by value across numeric types (i32, u32, f32, bool), and NaN never equals anything; strings compare by TEXT, so the two inputs' string tables need not agree. A string on one side and a number on the other is refused. Empty (the default) excludes nothing. |
| `excludeSelf` | bool | `false` |  |  |  | Skip target i for query i: for ONE cloud wired to both pins, asking whether each box meets any OTHER box of its own set. Refused when the two inputs have different point counts, since they cannot then be one cloud. With two different clouds of equal size it silently pairs them by index, so leave it off there. |
| `hitAttr` | string | `"contact"` |  |  |  | Name of the bool point attribute (tuple 1) receiving whether the query met at least one target. A REPORTING SLOT: an existing bool tuple-1 column of that name is reset and rewritten, one of any other shape is refused rather than destroyed. Empty writes no flag (then countAttr or targetAttr must be set). |
| `countAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | OPT-IN: name of the i32 point attribute (tuple 1) receiving how many DISTINCT targets the query met (exclusions not counted). Worth writing when the question is how crowded a box is rather than whether it is clear. A reporting slot on the same terms as hitAttr. Empty (the default) writes none. |
| `targetAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | OPT-IN: name of the i32 point attribute (tuple 1) receiving the INDEX, in the targets input, of the met target whose box centre is nearest the centre of the query box AS TESTED (after its margins), ties — distances equal within a band scaled like the centres' rounding (see the node description), so a rotation's rounding cannot decide one — to the lowest index, or -1 when none was met. Feed it to transferByIndex as `index` to read that target's own attributes — its label, its id, its colour. A reporting slot on the same terms as hitAttr. Empty (the default) writes none. |

## brickConnectivity

Reads BRICKS on a stud lattice — one per point, its world corners in two point attributes, as packBricks writes them — and says how they hold together: which clutch-connected piece each brick belongs to, and the build STEP at which it can be placed. Two bricks are joined when one sits directly on the other's top face with overlapping footprints, which is what a stud gripping the brick above does; side-by-side contact joins nothing. `island` numbers the pieces 0, 1, 2…, ordered by their lowest point IDENTITY (stored position bits plus `seed`), so it does not move when the input is shuffled. `step` is an ORDER YOU COULD BUILD IN, bottom up: the steps are the distinct bottom courses of the build in rising order, and at each one a brick is placed if it stands on the build's lowest course (the table) or clutches a brick already placed. A brick that hangs UNDER something — a plate held only by the floor laid on top of it — cannot be placed in its own course, because nothing is there to grip yet; it is placed in the step of the first brick above it that is, along with anything hanging under it in turn. So every brick, when it is placed, is attached to something already standing. A brick no order can attach — in a piece with no brick on the lowest course, which is a piece held by nothing — gets `step` -1, and its `island` is still numbered: the count of -1 steps is how many bricks a build would drop. Bricks must not overlap (two bricks claiming one cell are refused, naming both points) and every corner must land on the lattice, exactly as packBricks requires; a zero-volume box is not a brick and gets -1 for both. OUTPUT `out` is the input with two i32 point attributes added, `islandAttr` and `stepAttr`, the number of steps as a DETAIL u32 (`stepCountAttr`, the range a build-step slider runs over: steps 0 to stepCount - 1), and optionally the island count as a DETAIL u32; no point is moved, added or reordered, so a filterByExpression on `step` downstream shows the build as it stood after any step. INPUT `studs` (optional) takes packBricks' `studs` output — best with `studMode: 'all'` — whose `brickId` and `brickAbove` columns are indices into the bricks on `in`, so `in` must be that same packBricks' `out` with nothing dropped or reordered (an index past the end is refused, naming the stud). Wired, OUTPUT `studs` is those studs with two i32 point attributes added: `stepAttr`, the step of the brick the stud stands on (-1 when that brick is never attached), and `stepAboveAttr`, the step of the brick resting on it — -1 when there is none OR when that brick is never attached, both of which mean no partial build ever covers the stud — plus the same `stepCountAttr`. So 'stud is showing after step t' is `step` >= 0 and `step` <= t and (`stepAbove` < 0 or `stepAbove` > t), with no join downstream. Unwired, `studs` is an empty collection and `out` is unaffected. HALO: none — a piece is a fact about the whole build. Not GPU-eligible.

**Category:** attribute

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry), `studs` (geometry, optional)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry), `studs` (geometry)

**Examples:** [examples-brick-house.json](../graphs/examples-brick-house.json) — 1 total (`pcg graphs --node brickConnectivity`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `minAttr` | string | `"boundsMin"` |  |  |  | POINT attribute (f32 or i32, tuple size 3) holding each brick's minimum corner in WORLD units, as packBricks writes it. Refused, naming the point, unless every component lands on the lattice. |
| `maxAttr` | string | `"boundsMax"` |  |  |  | POINT attribute (f32 or i32, tuple size 3) holding each brick's maximum corner in world units, on the same terms as `minAttr`. |
| `pitch` | vec3 | `[1,0.4,1]` |  |  |  | World size of one lattice cell: [stud, plate, stud], the same lattice the bricks were packed on. x and z must be equal and every component positive. |
| `origin` | vec3 | `[0,0,0]` |  |  |  | World position of a lattice corner, as for packBricks. |
| `islandAttr` | string | `"island"` |  |  |  | POINT attribute (i32) receiving each brick's piece: 0 for the piece holding the lowest point identity, then 1, 2… in the same order. -1 on a zero-volume box. May not be empty. An existing column of another shape under this name is refused rather than replaced. |
| `stepAttr` | string | `"step"` |  |  |  | POINT attribute (i32) receiving each brick's build step: the index of the bottom course at which it is placed, counting only courses at which some brick is placed, so steps run 0, 1, 2… with no gaps. -1 for a brick no order can attach (its piece never reaches the lowest course) and for a zero-volume box. May not be empty, and may not equal `islandAttr`. With `studs` wired, the same name is written on each stud: the step of the brick it stands on. |
| `stepAboveAttr` | string | `"stepAbove"` |  |  |  | With `studs` wired, the POINT attribute (i32) receiving each stud's COVER step: the step of the brick resting on it (its `brickAbove`), so the stud stops showing once that step is built. -1 when no brick rests on it (`brickAbove` below 0) and when the one that does is never attached (step -1) — either way no partial build ever covers the stud, so `stepAbove` < 0 reads as 'never covered'. Must not be empty, equal `stepAttr`, or name `brickId` or `brickAbove`, the index columns it is read through. Ignored when `studs` is not wired. |
| `stepCountAttr` | string | `"stepCount"` |  |  |  | DETAIL attribute (u32) receiving the number of build steps — the highest step plus 1, or 0 when there are no bricks (an empty input, or only zero-volume boxes; a brick on the lowest course is always placed, at step 0) — on `out`, and on `studs` when it is wired. It is the range a host's build-step slider runs over: every step from 0 to stepCount - 1 places at least one brick. Empty writes nothing; may not equal `islandsAttr`. |
| `islandsAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | Optional DETAIL attribute (u32) receiving the number of pieces; 1 is a build that lifts as one. Empty (the default) writes nothing. The same count packBricks' own `islandsAttr` gives for the bricks it packed. |

## bridgePaths

Joins each polyline on `from` to one polyline on `to` with a strip of quads, point i to point i: segment i of the one path, segment i of the other and the two rungs between their ends make one quad, emitted as two 3-vertex 'poly' triangles (the mesh convention every consumer here reads — surfaceSample, the uv and raycast transfers). This is the band between two lines: a road shoulder between a kerb line and its pathShift offset, a ribbon between two rails, an annulus between two concentric rings, one band of a loft between two sections. PAIRING: with `pairAttr` empty the i-th polyline of `from` pairs with the i-th of `to`, in primitive order, and different path COUNTS are refused naming both; with `pairAttr` set, the path on each side carrying the same key is the pair. A pair's two paths must have the SAME number of points (refused naming both counts and both primitives — resample both with pathResample mode 'count' to one count) and must both be open or both closed: two closed paths wrap, so the last quad joins the last points back to the first and the band is a ring with no seam in its geometry. THE PATHS ARE NOT RESAMPLED OR MATCHED: point i meets point i whatever their spacing, so two paths sampled by the same count along similar lengths give an even band, and an offset path that folded back on itself gives a folded band. OUTPUT: every `from` point of every pair, then every `to` point, each carrying its own input's point attributes (a name both sides carry must agree on type and tuple size, and fills with its default where one side lacks it); a `normal` (f32 tuple 3) the node writes — shared by the two points of a rung, the normalized sum of the unit normals of the (at most two) quads meeting at that rung, so a band that bends shades round; a rung whose quads have no area or cancel borrows the nearest earlier rung that has one, or the first — and a `uv` (f32 tuple 2): `u` is each side's own normalized arc length, `v` is 0 on `from` and 1 on `to`. On a closed pair `u` returns to 0 across the closing quad, as it does on sweepProfile's closed tubes. Every triangle carries the primitive attributes of its `from` path; `to`'s primitive attributes and detail are dropped. FACING: with `from` on the right of the direction of travel and `to` on its left (seen from above), the band faces UP — the triangles wind so that (along `from`) x (from -> to) is the face normal — and `flip` turns it over. Single-sided like any mesh: flip it, or bridge the pair twice, for a band seen from both sides. `normal` and `uv` are reporting slots — an input already carrying either at a different shape is refused by name. Deterministic: pairs come out in `from` primitive order, quads in path order.

**Category:** surface

**Inputs:** `from` (geometry), `to` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-bridge-two-paths.json](../graphs/basics-bridge-two-paths.json) — 1 total (`pcg graphs --node bridgePaths`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `pairAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | Name of a scalar PRIMITIVE attribute, on BOTH inputs, that says which `to` path each `from` path is bridged to: the paths carrying the same key are a pair. A key is a whole number (setAttribute type 'i32' on the primitive domain) or a string compared by value — the rules pointsToPath's `groupAttr` sets. Every key must appear exactly once on each side; a key repeated on one side, or present on only one, is refused by name. Pairs still come out in `from` primitive order. Empty (the default) pairs the i-th polyline of `from` with the i-th of `to` — right whenever both sides came from the same paths through nodes that keep primitive order (pathShift, pathResample, transformPoints), and the reason this is optional. |
| `flip` | bool | `false` |  |  |  | Turn the band over: reverse every triangle's winding and negate the written `normal`, so the band faces the other way. The default faces (along `from`) x (from -> to): with `from` on the right of the direction of travel and `to` on its left, a flat band faces up. |

## connectByIndex

Builds one 2-vertex `polyline` per point, from that point to the point its `index` names — the edge set a PARENT POINTER describes. Built over the SAME points that arrived, so every point attribute survives and a junction is genuinely one point shared by every edge that meets there. This is the constructor for anything that GREW: a tree's branches, a root system, a river network, a dendrite, a crack. WHY IT IS A NODE AND NOT A RECIPE: `pointsToPath` is the only other path constructor and it PARTITIONS — `groupAttr` is read once per point and that point joins exactly one group — so its groups are vertex-disjoint chains and every point ends with degree at most 2. A star (1 -> 0, 2 -> 0, 3 -> 0) can emit at most two of its three edges under ANY assignment of group keys, because the centre belongs to one chain only. Branching is precisely the case that excludes. THE SHAPE IS NOT THE GAP — `connectPoints` already gives a point three incident edges and more, and this file's own header says a degree-5 point is expressible today. What no shipped node can do is be HANDED the answer: `connectPoints` asks its question in SPACE and decides the pairs itself, and `transferByIndex` IS handed an index but spends it on VALUES and promises it changes no topology. DIRECTION IS THE PAYLOAD, and this is where it differs from `connectPoints` deliberately: vertex 0 of each edge is the point that named the index and vertex 1 is the point it named, so child-to-parent is recoverable from the topology itself rather than needing a separate column. `connectPoints` orders its endpoints by key because a proximity edge has no direction to preserve; a parent pointer has nothing else. Edges come out in POINT ORDER, one per point that names a usable parent. A ROOT names `rootValue` (-1 by default, the sentinel `copyToPoints`' `targetIndexAttr` writes and `quotaRebalance` uses for 'stays') and contributes no edge — that is ordinary data, not a miss, which is why it is a separate param from `outOfRange`: a forest of several trees is a normal input here, and a node that could not say 'this one has no parent' could not describe one. THE INDEX IS AN ORDINAL, NOT AN IDENTITY, and it carries `transferByIndex`'s trap in a sharper form: a filter, a sort, a resample, a partition or a `mergePoints` anywhere between the node that COMPUTED the pointers and this one renumbers every point, and index 7 is then whatever landed seventh. There the damage is a wrong value; here it is a wrong EDGE, and a plausible-looking one. Compute the pointers and spend them with no renumbering step in between. PARTITIONING: an edge reads one stored index and the point it names, and consults no third point, so a cell holding both decides that edge exactly — emit it from the cell owning its FIRST vertex under the half-open rule and the cells tile the network with no duplicate and no gap, exactly as `connectPoints` documents. The halo is whatever bound the PRODUCER of the index column already needed, and is not this node's to invent; that is what separates this from the two things Stretch actually cut — CHAINING degree-2 runs into polylines, which is not partition-invariant at the primitive domain, and `findPath`, for which no finite halo suffices. (`maxDegree` itself is recorded there as halo-exact and merely unbuilt, not refused.)
