Output tags are the union of input tags.

**Category:** point op

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry, multi, optional)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-merge-points.json](../graphs/basics-merge-points.json), [basics-curve-shaping.json](../graphs/basics-curve-shaping.json), [basics-field-shaping.json](../graphs/basics-field-shaping.json) — 18 total (`pcg graphs --node mergePoints`)

**Params:** *(none)*

## mergePrimitives

Concatenates every connected geometry, in connection order, KEEPING TOPOLOGY: points, vertices and primitives are appended and each input's vertex and primitive references are renumbered onto its place in the result, so an authored network merged with a generated one comes out a single network. The topology-preserving twin of mergePoints, which carries points only and so turns any input into a bare cloud. The point, vertex and primitive domains each carry the union of that domain's attributes: an attribute missing on an input fills with its default over that input's range, and attributes sharing a name must agree on type and tuple size. The one exception is `primtype`, which is a type tag rather than a value: each input's primitives keep their own tag, and primitives from an input carrying no primtype column come out with an EMPTY tag rather than inheriting another input's — this node cannot know what an untagged primitive is and must not guess. One consequence to know before relying on absence: surfaceSample ignores the tag entirely when a geometry has NO primtype column, so an untagged triangle mesh samples fine alone but is skipped once a tagged input is merged in and the column exists — tag such a mesh upstream (createTriangleMesh does) rather than leaving it untagged. Mixed primitive types are allowed, because every consumer selects what it understands (surfaceSample takes 3-vertex `poly`, the path nodes take `polyline`). An input with no topology contributes its points and no primitives, which is not an error. The detail domain is not carried: every input has one and choosing between them would be a guess. Output tags are the union of input tags. Point identity is position bits plus the `seed` attribute and both are copied verbatim, so two inputs holding a point at the same position with the same seed are ONE point to every identity-keyed decision (jitter, probabilistic filters, randomField) — the same inherited hazard mergePoints carries. Give clouds that must stay distinct distinct seeds.

**Category:** point op

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry, multi, optional)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-merge-primitives.json](../graphs/basics-merge-primitives.json), [basics-cut-a-courtyard.json](../graphs/basics-cut-a-courtyard.json), [basics-gabriel-cells.json](../graphs/basics-gabriel-cells.json) — 14 total (`pcg graphs --node mergePrimitives`)

**Params:** *(none)*

## meshFromField

Turns a scalar FIELD into a triangle mesh: the surface where the field crosses `isoValue`. Other nodes build geometry from no input at all — meshPrimitive makes a box, pointGrid a lattice — but their SHAPE is written into the node; this is the only one whose shape and topology come out of an EXPRESSION, which is how a cave, a blob, an eroded cliff or a melted rock gets made, which is to say every shape that is easy to write as 'where is this quantity zero' and hard to model any other way. Output is 3-vertex `poly` primitives carrying P and a `normal` taken from the field's own GRADIENT, so the surface shades smoothly however coarse the lattice; there is no `uv`, deliberately, because an isosurface has no natural parameterisation and a planar one would be a lie on every vertical face — use triplanar shading, or transferAttribute with mapping 'nearest' from something that does have UVs. IT CONTOURS BY TETRAHEDRA, NOT CUBES. Marching cubes looks 8 corner signs up in a 256-entry table, several of whose cases are genuinely ambiguous — a face with two diagonal corners inside can be joined either way — so the table encodes a convention that neighbouring cubes must agree on or the mesh cracks. Splitting each cube into 6 tetrahedra removes the choice: 4 corners, no ambiguous case, watertight because there is nothing to get wrong rather than because a table is right. The cost is roughly twice the triangles for the same lattice, which is the trade being made. THE LATTICE IS ANCHORED TO THE WORLD, not to `boundsMin`. Cube (i, j, k) always spans [i * cellSize, (i+1) * cellSize) whatever bounds asked for it, so two windows that overlap sample the same corners at the same positions and agree bit for bit — the property `volumeSample` deliberately does not have, since its cell size is a request divided into the extent it was given. `cellSize` here is exact and the bounds are widened outward to whole cubes. An optional geometry input replaces the bounds params with that geometry's own point extents, which chooses the REGION and never the lattice: contour around a scatter and the cubes are still the world's cubes. PARTITIONING: a tetrahedron reads 4 corner samples and no neighbour's answer, so this is zero-hop; a cell of a partitioned world cooks the cubes its own bounds cover and the union tiles them, because a cube belongs to exactly one cell under a half-open rule on its INDEX. Nothing is welded — every triangle carries its own three points — so no cell ever depends on a number another cell chose. Two conditions are on the caller. The field must be a function of POSITION: `fraction` and `index` measure the population of this cook and `attribute` can only read the bookkeeping columns of a cloud this node manufactured, so all three are REFUSED by name (a field built with the code combinators carries no spec and cannot be screened, which makes it the author's promise instead). And the field must resolve on ONE path for a given world: CPU and GPU agree within a published tolerance, a corner classification is a COMPARE, and no tolerance bounds a comparison, so two cells that resolved the same corner differently can disagree about its sign and crack the seam.

**Category:** source

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry, optional)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-mesh-from-field.json](../graphs/basics-mesh-from-field.json) — 1 total (`pcg graphs --node meshFromField`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `field` | f32 | `0` |  |  | yes | The scalar field to contour. A plain number contours nothing — a constant never crosses anything — so this is a field param that only makes sense as a field. Any expression of position works: a noise for a cloud or a rock, `sub(length(position()), r)` for a sphere, the difference of two for a carve. IT MUST BE A FUNCTION OF POSITION AND THAT IS ENFORCED: `fraction`, `index` and `attribute` are refused by name, because the first two measure the population of this cook and the third can only reach the bookkeeping columns of the lattice this node builds. The refusal names the offending position inside the expression. A field composed with the CODE combinators carries no spec and so cannot be screened at all; that one is the author's promise, and breaking it does not throw, it makes a partitioned world disagree with itself at the seams. Resolved on the CPU over the lattice corners, in chunks — a partitioned world cooks under a deadline, and one resolve over every corner at once is a single uninterruptible call however small the budget. The chunking cannot move the answer precisely BECAUSE of the purity rule above: a field that reads only position cannot tell one split of the corners from another. |
| `isoValue` | f32 | `0` |  |  |  | The value the surface sits at. Moving it grows or shrinks the solid without re-sampling anything conceptually — for a signed distance it is a plain offset, and for a noise it is a threshold, which is why an eroded rock and a cave are the same graph with two settings. The surface passes EXACTLY through this value: a vertex is placed by linear interpolation along the lattice edge that crosses it, so evaluating the field at any emitted vertex returns this number to within the interpolation's own error, which is what `isosurface.test.ts` measures. |
| `cellSize` | f32 | `1` | >= 0 |  |  | Lattice cube edge in world units, and unlike volumeSample's it is EXACT rather than a request. The lattice is anchored at the world origin, so cube (i, j, k) spans [i * cellSize, (i+1) * cellSize) whatever bounds asked for it, and the bounds are widened outward to whole cubes rather than divided into them. That is the whole partitioning story in one sentence: two windows that overlap sample the same corners at the same world positions, so they agree bit for bit, where a lattice fitted to its own bounds would put its corners somewhere different in every window. Cost grows with the CUBE of the bounds over this value, and every sample is a field evaluation, so halving it is eight times the work. |
| `boundsMin` | vec3 | `[-8,-8,-8]` |  |  |  | Low corner of the region to contour, in world units, and IGNORED when a geometry is connected — the input's own point extents replace both bounds then. Widened outward to the enclosing whole cube, so the surface is never clipped mid-cube by a bound that fell inside one. Under a partitioned world this is the cell's own rectangle: bind it per cell, and because the lattice is world-anchored rather than bounds-anchored the cells agree about every corner they share. |
| `boundsMax` | vec3 | `[8,8,8]` |  |  |  | High corner of the region to contour, in world units, and IGNORED when a geometry is connected. HALF-OPEN: the last cube walked is the one ending at or before this face, so two cells whose rectangles meet on a cell-size multiple tile the surface instead of both emitting the seam. The low face is widened OUTWARD to a whole cube, so the contoured region can reach up to one cube below `boundsMin` — a solid that leaves the box is left open there rather than capped, since this node emits the surface it found and never invents a lid. Note also that the FIELD is evaluated one cube further out again on every face, because the gradient that gives the normals is a central difference and needs a corner beyond the ones it reports on. A field with a pole just outside the box therefore still refuses the cook, and moving the box is the fix. |
| `inside` | enum | `"below"` |  | `below`, `above` |  | Which side of `isoValue` is solid, which decides only two things: which way the surface faces, and hence which way the winding runs. 'below' (the default) treats field < isoValue as inside, the convention a signed distance uses — negative within the shape. 'above' is the one a density or an occupancy noise wants, where more means more material. The surface geometry is identical either way; flipping this turns it inside out rather than moving it. |

## meshPrimitive

Builds a parametric triangle mesh with no input: shape 'plane' is one axis-aligned rectangle, shape 'box' is six of them around a volume, shape 'cylinder' is an upright (y-axis) cylinder with optional caps, and shape 'disc' is one flat polygonal disc lying like a plane. Output carries P (f32 tuple 3), a uv point attribute (f32 tuple 2) running 0..1 across each face, one 3-vertex 'poly' primitive per triangle — everything surfaceSample, transferAttribute's 'uv' and 'raycast' mappings, and promoteAttribute need — and, with `normals` on, a `normal` point attribute (f32 tuple 3). This is the only mesh source that survives serialization: dataInput's items are injected at runtime and a saved graph carries none, so a graph that must cook from JSON alone gets its surface from here. Plane and disc normals point along the positive third axis (+y for orientation 'xz', +z for 'xy', +x for 'yz'); box and cylinder normals point outward. A plane or box face's point order is u fastest then v, a box one block per face in the order +x, -x, +y, -y, +z, -z; a box's faces do not share points, so uv seams are exact. A cylinder is its wall block — `subdivisions.y + 1` rings of `sides + 1` points, round the ring fastest, bottom ring first, the seam column duplicated so u runs 0..1 — then the top cap's `sides` corners, then the bottom cap's; caps share no points with the wall, so the rim is a real crease, and each cap (like a disc) is a fan of `sides - 2` triangles from its first corner with no centre point. Ring corners lie ON the ellipse whose diameters are the size's components, starting on +z and turning toward +x, so a `sides` that is a multiple of 4 reaches the full `size` extents exactly. A zero size component makes degenerate (zero-area) triangles, which area-weighted sampling and the raycast mapping skip.

**Category:** source

**Inputs:** *(none)*

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-mesh-primitive.json](../graphs/basics-mesh-primitive.json), [basics-surface-sample.json](../graphs/basics-surface-sample.json), [basics-promote-attribute.json](../graphs/basics-promote-attribute.json) — 22 total (`pcg graphs --node meshPrimitive`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `shape` | enum | `"plane"` |  | `plane`, `box`, `cylinder`, `disc` |  | 'plane' builds one rectangle in the world plane named by `orientation`; 'box' builds a closed six-sided box of the full `size` extents (and ignores `orientation`); 'cylinder' builds a `sides`-sided cylinder standing on the y axis, `size.y` tall, closed at the ends `caps` names (it ignores `orientation` — rotate it downstream to lay it down); 'disc' builds one flat `sides`-sided disc in the plane named by `orientation`, the round counterpart of 'plane'. A disc is its own shape rather than a zero-height cylinder because a flat cylinder is the wrong mesh for it: its wall would be a ring of `2 * sides` zero-area triangles per band, and with both caps on it would be two coincident discs facing opposite ways. |
| `size` | vec3 | `[10,10,10]` | >= 0 |  |  | Full extents along world x, y, z, in world units (not half-extents). For 'plane' and 'disc', the component along the normal axis is ignored. For 'cylinder' and 'disc' the two in-plane components are DIAMETERS — x and z for a cylinder, whose height is y — so unequal ones make an elliptic section; a stud of radius 0.3 is size [0.6, height, 0.6]. |
| `center` | vec3 | `[0,0,0]` |  |  |  | World position of the shape's center. |
| `orientation` | enum | `"xz"` |  | `xz`, `xy`, `yz` |  | Which world plane a 'plane' or a 'disc' lies in, and so which axis is its normal: 'xz' is the ground plane (normal +y), 'xy' faces +z, 'yz' faces +x. Ignored for 'box' and 'cylinder'. |
| `subdivisions` | vec3 | `[1,1,1]` | >= 1 |  |  | Quads along world x, y, z — whole numbers, minimum 1. A plane uses the two components of its own axes ([1,1,1] is a single quad, two triangles); a box uses all three, each face taking the two that span it; a cylinder uses only y, the number of bands its wall is cut into up its height (round it, `sides` counts instead); a disc uses none. More subdivisions give finer sampling and a finer uv/raycast target. |
| `sides` | i32 | `16` | 3..256 |  |  | Corners round a 'cylinder' or a 'disc' — whole number, 3..256; ignored by 'plane' and 'box'. The section is the regular polygon INSCRIBED in the ellipse (its corners lie on it), the first corner on +z, so 3 is a triangular prism, 4 a diamond, and a multiple of 4 reaches the full `size` extents exactly. Cost is linear: each wall ring is `sides + 1` points (the extra one duplicates the seam for the uv), each band `2 * sides` triangles, and each cap `sides` points and `sides - 2` triangles. The same name and range as sweepProfile's circle `sides`; the default is 16 rather than its 8 because that one matches a shipped tube asset and this one is meant to read round on its own. |
| `caps` | enum | `"both"` |  | `none`, `top`, `bottom`, `both` |  | Which ends of a 'cylinder' are closed, in extrudePolygon's spelling: 'both' is a closed solid (every edge shared by exactly two triangles once the coincident rim and seam points are welded), 'bottom' alone is a cup, 'top' alone a tube with a lid, 'none' is an open tube. Ignored by every other shape. A cap faces out of the cylinder (+y on top, -y below) and shares no points with the wall, so the rim stays a hard crease and the cap's planar uv is independent of the wall's. |
| `flip` | bool | `false` |  |  |  | Reverse every triangle's winding (and the written `normal` with it, under `normals`), which flips the surface — a plane or a disc becomes a ceiling, a box an inward-facing room, a cylinder an inward-facing shaft. |
| `normals` | bool | `false` |  |  |  | Write a `normal` point attribute (f32 tuple 3): FLAT on every flat face — one face's unit axis on each of that face's points, derived from the same winding the triangles use (so `flip` turns both) — the split-corner shading sweepProfile's 'square' and extrudePolygon give their hard edges, which a box's unshared face points make exact; a cylinder's caps and a disc are flat the same way. A cylinder's WALL is the one curved face, and gets its SMOOTH analytic normal instead (radial on a circle, the ellipse's own normal on an elliptic section), so it shades round with no smoothNormals pass while its rim, whose cap points are separate, stays a hard edge. That holds however few `sides` it has: a 3- or 6-sided prism with `normals` on shades ROUND, not faceted, and so does the renderer's own computed normal with `normals` off, because the wall's points are shared round the ring. A faceted prism needs split corners, which this node does not build: extrudePolygon over a polygon outline gives one. TURN IT ON when this mesh is merged with one that carries a normal (sweepProfile, extrudePolygon, meshFromField): mergePrimitives fills a column missing on one input with its default, (0, 0, 0), and a lit material renders those points black. OFF BY DEFAULT because a stored normal is only right until something moves P, and the renderer computes one from the triangles when none is stored: the commonest use of a plane is displacement into terrain, which would then shade flat on rolling ground, and a box copied onto ROTATED targets keeps its unturned normal unless copyToPoints' `rotateAttrs` names `normal`. A graph that displaces the mesh and still wants a stored normal recomputes it afterwards with smoothNormals. |

## occlusionCull

Removes points whose ORIENTED BOX stands in a line of sight, after first trying to move them out of it. Its subject is three things and nothing else: a cloud of EYE POSES, the TARGETS visible from each of them, and a cloud of boxes that may block the lines between — so it serves a driver who must see the road ahead, a guard who must see the gate, and a camera that must see the actor, with no knowledge of roads, gates or actors in it. The `in` pin carries the boxes: each point's `P` is the box centre, its `rot` (f32x4) the orientation and its `scale` (f32x3) the FULL extents, which are the same three columns spawnInstances reads, so what is tested is what will be drawn. The `sight` pin carries the lines: its points are the eye positions, raised by `eyeOffset`, and the path through them supplies the targets. FROM EACH EYE THE TEST IS A FAN OF CHORDS, not one chord to the far end, and that is the difference between a rule that works and one that passes vacuously: the requirement is that the next `lookAhead` of PATH be visible, and a box can leave the far point in plain view while standing in front of everything between — a single chord to the end of the run would clear it. `samples` chords are tested per eye, to points evenly spaced in ARC LENGTH along the run ahead. The test itself runs in each box's OWN frame (the slab method over its three axes), never in the world: a box turned into the sight line presents a narrower profile than its world-aligned hull, and on a straight the two agree while through a bend they do not — which is precisely where the rule matters, so testing the hull would check the one case that never fails. `include` DECIDES WHETHER A POINT IS ASKED AT ALL, and a point it switches off is carried to the output unmoved and IN ITS PLACE — never tested, never pushed, never dropped — which is how a class of geometry that is STRUCTURE rather than scenery stays out of a rule about what may stand in a sight line: a tunnel's ribs span the road by construction, and 'clearing' one by shoving it aside opens a hole in the roof it is part of. Doing that with a filter and a merge instead would CONCATENATE, so the exempt points would come out at the end of the cloud and every index after them would shift, which for a cloud copyToPoints or spawnInstances reads in order is a different scene. A blocked point is PUSHED before it is dropped: the node steps it along `pushAxis` in `pushStep` increments up to `pushMax`, away from the sight path, and keeps the first position that clears every chord. Only a point that cannot be cleared by pushing is removed. That order is the point of the node and not an optimisation — pushing PRESERVES THE POPULATION COUNT, so any rule upstream that budgeted a number of points still has that number, and the asset the author placed is still in the scene; dropping spends both. With the default `pushMax` of 0 nothing is pushed and every blocker is dropped, which is the conservative reading: this node will not silently relocate an authored point by a distance nobody chose. DETERMINISM: points are visited in an order fixed by point IDENTITY — the bits of the stored position plus the per-point `seed` attribute — and never by array index, so shuffling the input, filtering something upstream, or deriving the same points inside another cell's halo yields the identical survivor set. Order matters here only when `pushClearance` is above 0, which makes a pushed point avoid the points already settled and so makes one verdict depend on others; below that every point is decided from the sight input alone. HALO: with `pushClearance` at 0 the answer for a point depends on the eyes within roughly `lookAhead + pushMax` of it and on nothing else, so a partitioned cook is exact given a window that wide. Above 0 it is a GREEDY op, and a greedy op has no finite width — this point settled here because that one settled there, an unbounded chain no halo covers — so a per-cell cook will differ from a whole-region one, and the difference shows up as pushed points overlapping at the seams rather than as an error. OUTPUT is a point cloud of the survivors with every point attribute carried, and the input's topology is NOT preserved under any setting. There is no `topology: "keep"` here, unlike the five point filters, for a reason those five do not have: this node MOVES points as well as removing them, and a primitive kept over a moved point would describe a shape nobody authored — a road that follows its lamp posts sideways. Rebuild any network downstream (pointsToPath, connectPoints). One consequence worth stating because nothing else will say it: a PUSHED point comes out with a different `P`, and `P` is half of a point's identity, so anything identity-keyed downstream (filterByDensity's probabilistic mode, selfPrune's tiebreak, randomField) re-rolls for exactly the points this node moved. COST is one occlusion test per (point, nearby eye, sample), so it scales with the eye density of the sight path as well as with the cloud — resample the path to the spacing the rule actually needs rather than to the spacing it happens to have.

**Category:** filter

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry), `sight` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-sightline-cull.json](../graphs/basics-sightline-cull.json) — 1 total (`pcg graphs --node occlusionCull`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `include` | f32 | `1` |  |  | yes | Nonzero (the default) puts the point IN the test: its box is checked against every chord it can reach, and it is pushed or dropped on the answer. Zero takes it out entirely — no chord is ever tested against it, and it is carried to the output UNMOVED AND IN ITS PLACE, between the same two neighbours it arrived between. THAT LAST PART IS WHY THIS IS A PARAM AND NOT SOMETHING A CALLER CAN SPELL WITH A FILTER. Holding a class of points out of this node by filtering them off and merging them back concatenates: the exempt ones arrive at the END of the cloud, every index after the first of them shifts, and a cloud that copyToPoints or spawnInstances reads in order becomes a different scene — so the only way to say 'do not test this point' without also saying 'and put it somewhere else' is here. IT IS NOT A SECOND SPELLING OF `pushMax` 0, and the two cannot compete because they answer different questions: `pushMax` 0 says 'drop this one rather than move it', which is a verdict on a point that WAS tested and did block, while this says the question is never asked — so for an excluded point `pushAxis` and `pushMax` are never read, and it can be neither pushed nor dropped no matter what they say. An excluded point is never a blocker, so nothing about how blockers are handled reaches it. It does still OCCUPY its position: `pushClearance` counts it among the points already settled, exactly as it counts a point that was tested and cleared, because a pushed point has to avoid what is there rather than what was tested. As a FIELD it is evaluated on the `in` points, one flag per point, which is the form this is really for — attribute("cover"), or a comparison against a class column, excludes a KIND of thing rather than a list of indices, and the expression goes on meaning the right thing after the cloud upstream changes length. A non-finite value is refused with the param named, as every field-capable param in this node is: a NaN gate is a broken expression rather than a decision, and it is deliberately not filterByExpression's reading, where NaN means drop. NOTHING IS REPORTED, which a reader has to know before relying on the output: this node writes no column saying which points it tested, so a point it never asked about and a point it asked and cleared are indistinguishable on the way out — both come out exactly where they started. The `include` expression IS the record of the difference, it is one the caller already has, and asking it again is cheaper than a column this node would have to write on every cook for the callers that do not care. |
| `lookAhead` | f32 | `12` | >= 0 |  | yes | How far along the sight path, in world units of ARC LENGTH, must be visible from each eye. The fan's targets are spread evenly over that distance, so this and `samples` together fix the resolution of the test: `lookAhead / samples` is the gap between consecutive targets, and a box narrower than that gap can slip between two chords and be kept. Size them together — halving the gap doubles the cost. As a FIELD it is evaluated on the SIGHT input's points, one look-ahead per eye, which is how a rule that varies along the route is written: attribute("speed") scaled to a stopping distance asks for more sight where the observer is moving faster and less where a tight corner makes the demand impossible to satisfy anyway. A value of 0 leaves the fan with nowhere to go and collapses every chord to the eye position itself, which still asks one question — is the eye INSIDE the box — and nothing more; that is the honest reading of a zero-length line of sight, not an off switch. Negative values are read the same way as 0. A look-ahead longer than the path itself simply clamps at the far end on an open run and wraps on a closed one. This is also the HALO WIDTH a partitioned cook needs, plus `pushMax` and plus the widest box half-diagonal; as a field, use the bound the expression can reach ANYWHERE in the world rather than the largest value present in the cell, which is circular — the cell's cloud has already been clipped by the very halo you are sizing. |
| `samples` | u32 | `8` | 1..1024 |  |  | How many chords make up one eye's fan. The targets sit at `lookAhead * i / samples` for i in 1..samples, so the far end of the run is always tested and the eye's own position never is. The count exists because the rule is about a STRETCH of path rather than a point on it: with samples 1 the test is a single chord to the far end, which a box standing squarely across the middle of the run does not touch — it leaves the far point visible while hiding everything before it, and the cull would keep it. Raise this until the gap between targets is smaller than the narrowest box you care about; lowering it is the cheapest way to make the node faster and the first thing to make it wrong. The ceiling of 1024 is a guard on cost rather than a semantic limit, and the total number of chords (eyes times samples) is bounded again at cook time. |
| `eyeOffset` | vec3 | `[0,1.5,0]` |  |  | yes | World-space offset added to each eye's position on the sight path, in world units. The default raises the eye 1.5 above the path, which is where an observer's eye is relative to the ground they stand on — the reason the param exists at all is that the path is almost always the surface and almost never the viewpoint, and testing from the surface would have every kerb and every rut block the view. The TARGETS are not offset: they stay on the path, because what must be visible is the path itself, and lifting both ends would let a low box hide under a chord that clears it. As a FIELD it is evaluated on the SIGHT input's points, one offset per eye, so a route that climbs into a vehicle and back out again can carry its own eye height; build it with vec(x, y, z), and note that a scalar FIELD broadcasts to all three axes (rarely what an eye height means) while a plain scalar is refused outright. An offset of [0, 0, 0] puts the eye exactly on the path. |
| `pushAxis` | vec3 | `[1,0,0]` |  |  | yes | Direction along which a blocked point is moved before the node gives up and drops it, in world units. Normalized internally, so only its direction is read; the SIGN is chosen by the node, not by the author — a point is pushed in whichever of ±axis takes it FURTHER from the nearest eye, because 'out of the way' has no other meaning when the sight line is the only thing there is to be out of the way of. That is what lets one axis serve both sides of a route without an author writing a sign per point. As a FIELD it is evaluated on the `in` points, one direction per point, which is the form this param is really for: a lateral written upstream by pathSegments or promoted off the road primitive gives every point the outward direction of the stretch it stands on, and a straight world axis stops being an approximation the moment the route turns. A ZERO axis cannot be pushed along, so such a point is dropped the moment it blocks — which is a deliberate way to spell 'this one must never move' for a piece whose whole value is being in line with its neighbours (a row of markers with one shoved out of line reads as a mistake; the same row two markers shorter still reads as a row). `pushMax` 0 says the same thing for the whole cloud. |
| `pushMax` | f32 | `0` | >= 0 |  | yes | Furthest a blocked point may be moved along `pushAxis`, in world units. The default of 0 means NEVER PUSH: every blocker is dropped, and the node is a pure cull. There is deliberately no generous default distance — 6 is a long way in a corridor and nothing at all on a motorway, so a default would either do nothing or silently relocate an authored point by an amount nobody chose, and of the two failures dropping the point is the one an author notices. Set it as soon as the population count matters: pushing keeps the point, keeps the asset, and keeps whatever budget upstream counted, where dropping spends all three. As a FIELD it is evaluated on the `in` points, one allowance per point, and 0 for a point is exactly the 'drop rather than move' exception — attribute("locked") driving it lets a class of props be immovable while everything around them shuffles aside. The search is a ladder of `pushStep` increments and stops at the FIRST distance that clears every chord, so the point moves the least it can rather than the most it may; a point still blocked at `pushMax` is dropped. Widening this widens the halo a partitioned cook needs by the same amount, since a point can be tested against eyes that far beyond where it started. |
| `pushStep` | f32 | `0.5` | >= 0 |  |  | Distance between successive push attempts, in world units. Must be greater than 0 — a step of 0 is a search that never advances, and it is refused rather than silently treated as 'do not push', which is what `pushMax` 0 already says. This is a RESOLUTION knob and not a distance the author cares about: it decides how close to the minimum the found position is, so a point that would clear at 1.2 is placed at 1.5 with the default step and at 1.25 with a step of 0.25. It is deliberately not field-capable — a per-point search resolution is a knob nobody needs and would make two points that started together end up on different ladders, which is a difference in the OUTPUT that no author asked for. Every attempt costs a full sweep of the point's eyes, so the ratio pushMax / pushStep is the per-point cost multiplier and is bounded: a ratio above 4096 is refused with both numbers named. |
| `pushClearance` | f32 | `0` | >= 0 |  | yes | Room a PUSHED point needs at its new position, in world units, measured to the points already settled. It exists because pushing along a shared axis piles blockers up: a dozen props shoved aside from a dozen different eyes all come to rest at the same offset, interpenetrating, and the cull that was supposed to tidy the scene has made a heap in it. With a clearance set, a pushed point skips a rung of the ladder that would land it inside a neighbour and keeps climbing. THIS IS WHAT MAKES THE NODE GREEDY, and the trade is worth stating plainly: at 0 (the default) every point's verdict depends only on the sight input and on the point itself, so the node is embarrassingly parallel, exactly reproducible from a bounded halo, and completely indifferent to visit order. Above 0 a verdict depends on where other points settled, which depends on where THEIR neighbours settled, and no halo width covers that chain — a per-cell cook will disagree with a whole-region one at the seams. The visit order is fixed by point identity either way, so the answer is at least always the same answer. Only PUSHED points are tested: a point that never had to move stays exactly where the author put it and is never dropped for standing too close to something, which is selfPrune's job and not this node's. As a FIELD it is evaluated on the `in` points, so a large prop can demand more room than a small one; it is read on the point being PUSHED, not on the neighbour it is avoiding. |

## offsetBox

MOVES THE FACES of every cell's box OUT, or IN where the offset is negative — the scope offset of a shape grammar, the padding of a bounding box, the inset/outset of a face extrude — and changes nothing else: same points, same order, same seeds, every attribute and the topology carried, only `boundsMin`/`boundsMax` rewritten. A CELL IS A POINT WITH A BOX, as in splitBox: its standard boundsMin/boundsMax, read and written in the coordinates they hold; P, rot and scale are not read, so a ROTATED cell is offset in its stored box exactly as an unrotated one. THE OFFSETS are boxContact's margins, with boxContact's names and meaning, applied to the box itself instead of to a query: `margin` moves BOTH faces of each axis out (the uniform shorthand — [0.5, 0.5, 0.5] pads the box half a unit all round, [-0.5, -0.5, -0.5] insets it, and a scalar FIELD broadcasts to all three), `marginMin` moves the face on the MIN side of each axis and `marginMax` the face on the MAX side, signed and on top of `margin`: the min face moves out by margin + marginMin, the max face by margin + marginMax, so marginMin -d with marginMax +d SHIFTS the box d, and a box name in a field reads the box as it ARRIVED (marginMax vec(0, 1 - box("y1"), 0) puts the top face at y = 1 wherever it was). All three are vec3, plain or fields resolved per cell. THE FRAME (`frame`, placeBox's) says what the three components are. "world" (the default): [x, y, z], min side = the boundsMin corner. "face": the cell's OWN axes [along, up, out], read from the frame shellSplit writes on every piece and splitBox carries onto every piece cut from one — `alongAxisAttr` (the axis code, 0 = x, 1 = y, 2 = z) and `alongSignAttr` for along, `outwardAttr` (the outward normal) for out, and up DERIVED as the axis that keeps along x up = out, shellSplit's right-handed frame, so it need not be written. In "face" the MIN side of a component is the end its axis runs FROM and the MAX side the end it runs TO: marginMax [0, 0, 1] pushes the OUTER face out one unit and marginMin [0, 0, 1] the inner face in, on every wall and the floor and ceiling alike — one rule for a sill standing out of whichever face is outside; marginMin [1, 0, 0] grows the LEFT end as seen from outside; marginMax [0, 1, 0] raises the top. The frame is SELECTED, never inferred from what attributes happen to be present: under "face" a missing frame attribute is refused, naming every one missing, and a cell whose outward normal is not one signed axis (exactly one nonzero component — shellSplit's interior, whose normal is zero, has no out) or whose along axis is not 0, 1 or 2, is the out axis, or has a zero sign, is refused naming the cell; under "world" the frame attributes are not read at all. SNAP (`snap`, a lattice step per WORLD axis [x, y, z], 0 = none, placeBox's) puts the offset box on a lattice afterwards, every face of each snapped axis. `snapTo` "lattice" (the default) moves each face onto the ABSOLUTE lattice anchored at 0, as splitBox's snap, so neighbouring cells agree on one lattice; "size" rounds the box's SIZE on that axis to a whole number of steps and keeps its CENTRE, so a footprint grown by a margin and then made a multiple of 8 stays centred on what it grew from (a baseplate of a stock size under a lot). `snapMode` is the same five words on every box node: "round" (the default) goes to the nearest line (or size; a half rounds up), "floor" and "ceil" move every face down or up the WORLD axis (the size down or up), "out" GROWS onto the lattice (min face down, max face up; size up), "in" SHRINKS onto it (the reverse; size down). A face within f32 precision of a line counts as on it. COLLAPSE (`fit`): an inset past the box's own size crosses the faces, and a box written inside out is a defect every box node downstream refuses, so the default "error" refuses the cook, naming the cell, the axis, the crossed faces and the offsets; "scale" shrinks the INWARD moves in proportion until the two faces meet (a face that moved out keeps its place, so a one-sided inset past the far face stops ON it), leaving a box of no volume on that axis — and after a lattice snap "in" the meeting point is that proportion, not a lattice line; "allow" writes the crossed box as computed, as setBounds would. FIT GLOSSARY, one meaning per word across splitBox, shellSplit, placeBox and offsetBox: "error" refuses the cook, naming the cell and the numbers; "allow" writes the result as computed; "scale" rescales what does not fit, in proportion, until it fits exactly; "clamp" slides the box back inside, keeping its size, and cuts a box larger than its limit to the limit; "clip" cuts away what passes the limit and keeps the rest, nothing moved or rescaled. This node takes "error", "scale", "allow". Faces on an unsnapped axis that cross by no more than two f32 ulps at their coordinates MEET under "error" and "scale", put on one stored number; a crossing any larger is a real one, and faces on a snapped lattice axis are lattice lines, so any crossing there is a whole step. Under `snapTo` "size" a size that rounds to no whole step leaves a box of no volume at its centre: that is not a crossing, so `fit` has nothing to settle. An input box already inside out, or not finite, is refused under "error" and "scale". `where` leaves chosen cells exactly as they arrived (0 = untouched, no offset, snap or check), e.g. attributeIs("piece", "sill"). The output keeps every point: a box offset to nothing is a box of no volume, never a dropped point. CPU ONLY, and not a GPU adopter, for boxContact's reason: the offsets decide comparisons — which lattice line a face lands on, whether two faces crossed — and a device-rounded offset one ulp off moves a face a whole lattice step. Use it where a setBounds rebuilt a corner from box("lo") or box("hi") plus an offset: the face moved is then named rather than reconstructed.

**Category:** point op

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [examples-brick-house.json](../graphs/examples-brick-house.json) — 1 total (`pcg graphs --node offsetBox`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `margin` | vec3 | `[0,0,0]` |  |  | yes | How far BOTH faces of each axis move OUT, per component — [x, y, z] under frame "world", [along, up, out] under "face" — in the box's own units: the uniform shorthand. [1, 1, 1] pads the box one unit all round, [-1, -1, -1] insets it, [2, 0, 2] grows a footprint two units each way and leaves its height. As a FIELD it is resolved per cell on the INPUT (box names read the box as it arrived) and a scalar broadcasts to all three components. Adds to marginMin and marginMax. Must be finite. |
| `marginMin` | vec3 | `[0,0,0]` |  |  | yes | How far the MIN-SIDE face of each axis moves OUT, on top of `margin`, signed (negative pulls it in): under frame "world" the face the boundsMin corner is on, so marginMin [0, 1, 0] lowers the floor one unit; under "face" the end each frame axis runs FROM — the left end along (as seen from outside, on a shellSplit wall), the bottom up, the INNER face out. With marginMax it can shift the box (marginMin -d, marginMax +d moves it d). boxContact's marginMin, with its meaning. As a FIELD it is resolved per cell on the input, and may read the box names. Must be finite. |
| `marginMax` | vec3 | `[0,0,0]` |  |  | yes | How far the MAX-SIDE face of each axis moves OUT, on top of `margin`, signed: under frame "world" the face the boundsMax corner is on; under "face" the end each frame axis runs TO — the right end along, the top up, the OUTER face out, so marginMax [0, 0, 1] stands a sill one unit out of whichever face of its wall is outside. boxContact's marginMax, with its meaning. As a FIELD it is resolved per cell on the input, and may read the box names: marginMax vec(0, 1 - box("y1"), 0) puts the top at y = 1. Must be finite. |
| `frame` | enum | `"world"` |  | `world`, `face` |  | What the three components of the margins mean — placeBox's `frame`, with its meaning. "world": world axes [x, y, z], min side the boundsMin corner — the frame attributes are not read. "face": the cell's own axes [along, up, out] from the frame shellSplit writes (`alongAxisAttr`, `alongSignAttr`, `outwardAttr`; up is derived, completing the right-handed frame along x up = out), min side the end each axis runs from — so one offset reads the same on every wall, floor and ceiling of a shell. Selected here, never inferred: under "face" a missing frame attribute is refused, naming every one missing. Decided once per cook. |
| `alongAxisAttr` | string | `"alongAxis"` |  |  |  | Read only under frame "face": the tuple-1 numeric point attribute holding each cell's ALONG axis code, 0 = x, 1 = y, 2 = z — shellSplit's `alongAxisAttr`, carried by splitBox onto every piece. Any other value, or the out axis itself, is refused naming the cell. |
| `alongSignAttr` | string | `"alongSign"` |  |  |  | Read only under frame "face": the tuple-1 numeric point attribute holding the direction ALONG runs, its sign read (+1 or -1; shellSplit's `alongSignAttr`, left to right as seen from outside on a wall). Zero — what shellSplit leaves on a cell its `where` passed through — has no direction and is refused naming the cell, and so is a non-finite value. |
| `outwardAttr` | string | `"outward"` |  |  |  | Read only under frame "face": the tuple-3 numeric point attribute holding each cell's OUTWARD normal — shellSplit's `outwardAttr`. It must be ONE signed axis (exactly one nonzero component; its sign is the side), which is what makes the out component of a margin a face of the box; shellSplit's interior, whose normal is [0, 0, 0], and any tilted normal, are refused naming the cell. |
| `snap` | vec3 | `[0,0,0]` | >= 0 |  |  | A LATTICE STEP per WORLD axis [x, y, z] — in every frame, since a lattice is the world's — applied after the offset to every face of each axis whose step is not 0 (0 = no snapping, the default). How depends on `snapTo` and `snapMode`. splitBox's and placeBox's snap: snap [1, 0.4, 1] keeps a box on a brick lattice of 1-stud by 1-plate cells. |
| `snapTo` | enum | `"lattice"` |  | `lattice`, `size` |  | What `snap` rounds — placeBox's `snapTo`. "lattice": each face onto the ABSOLUTE lattice anchored at 0, as splitBox's snap, so neighbouring boxes share lines. "size": the box's SIZE on the axis to a whole number of steps, keeping its CENTRE — the box grows (or shrinks) equally at both faces, which is how a lot padded two studs each way becomes a stock 8-stud-multiple baseplate centred under it (margin [2, 0, 2], snap [8, 0, 8], snapTo "size", snapMode "out"). Under "size" an axis whose size is already negative is left for `fit`, and a size that rounds to no whole step is a box of no volume at its centre. Decided once per cook. |
| `snapMode` | enum | `"round"` |  | `round`, `floor`, `ceil`, `out`, `in` |  | Which way a face (or a size) lands on the `snap` lattice — the same five words on every box node. "round" (the default): the nearest line or size, a half rounding up. "floor" / "ceil": every face down / up the WORLD axis, both the same way (the size down / up). "out": the box GROWS onto it — the min face to the line at or below it, the max face to the line at or above it, the size up — so a padded box never loses what the offset gave it. "in": it SHRINKS onto it (the reverse), which can cross the faces of a box with no whole step inside it, for `fit` to settle. Ignored where the step is 0. Decided once per cook. |
| `fit` | enum | `"error"` |  | `error`, `scale`, `allow` |  | What happens when an inset (or an inward snap) crosses a box's two faces on an axis. "error" (the default) refuses the cook, naming the cell, the axis, the faces and the offsets — a box written inside out is refused by every box node downstream, so it is caught where it is made. "scale" shrinks the INWARD moves of the two faces in proportion until they meet, which leaves the box no volume on that axis — a symmetric inset past the middle stops at the centre, and a face that moved OUT keeps its place, so a one-sided inset past the far face stops on it. "allow" writes the crossed box as computed, as setBounds would. FIT GLOSSARY, one meaning per word across splitBox, shellSplit, placeBox and offsetBox: "error" refuses the cook, naming the cell and the numbers; "allow" writes the result as computed; "scale" rescales what does not fit, in proportion, until it fits exactly; "clamp" slides the box back inside, keeping its size, and cuts a box larger than its limit to the limit; "clip" cuts away what passes the limit and keeps the rest, nothing moved or rescaled. This node takes "error", "scale", "allow". On an unsnapped axis, faces crossing by no more than two f32 ulps at their coordinates MEET under "error" and "scale", on one stored number. An input box that is already inside out or not finite, and an offset that takes a face past what f32 bounds hold, are refused under "error" and "scale", and written as computed under "allow". Decided once per cook. |
| `where` | f32 | `1` |  |  | yes | Which cells are offset: nonzero offsets the cell, 0 leaves it EXACTLY as it arrived — no offset, no snap, no fit check, and its frame values not read (under frame "face" the frame attributes must still exist, so their columns can be looked up once). As a field it is resolved per cell, e.g. attributeIs("piece", "sill"). A non-finite value is refused naming the cell. |

## orientAlongVector

Sets the standard rot point attribute (f32 tuple 4 quaternion, [x, y, z, w]) so the chosen local axis points along `direction`, with `up` fixing the roll. The quaternion is right-handed and matches the spawner path's three.js Matrix4.compose conventions (and quatFromEulerDeg's frame), so with the default '+z' axis, spawned assets face the direction the way the spline-fence example's tangent yaw does. For axes ±x and ±z the local +Y axis turns as close to `up` as the direction allows; for axes ±y (which consume the up-like axis) local +Z takes that role. Points with a zero-length direction keep their existing rot (identity when the attribute is newly created). When direction and up are parallel or antiparallel (cross product squared length <= 1e-12, after normalizing both), the up hint deterministically falls back to [0, 0, 1], then [1, 0, 0].

**Category:** point op

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-orient-along-vector.json](../graphs/basics-orient-along-vector.json), [basics-orient-along-path.json](../graphs/basics-orient-along-path.json), [basics-where-bars-cross.json](../graphs/basics-where-bars-cross.json) — 13 total (`pcg graphs --node orientAlongVector`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `direction` | vec3 | `[0,0,1]` |  |  | yes | World-space direction the chosen local axis should point along; need not be unit length. Field-capable (resolved per point on the input, e.g. a tangent attribute; tuple 1 broadcasts). Zero-length directions leave that point's rot unchanged (identity when the rot attribute did not exist before). |
| `up` | vec3 | `[0,1,0]` |  |  | yes | Up hint fixing the roll around the direction; need not be unit length. When parallel/antiparallel to the direction (or zero), deterministically falls back to [0, 0, 1], then [1, 0, 0]. Field-capable (resolved per point on the input; tuple 1 broadcasts). A per-point up is what a curve that turns over needs: a CONSTANT up flips the roll a half turn as the direction passes through it, and everything placed along the curve snaps round with it — feed writeCurveFrame's `curveNormal` here instead and the roll varies smoothly. A field `up` keeps this node OFF the device-resident path, because the apply kernel bakes the normalized up in as a constant; the cook reports that by name in its fallbacks rather than silently producing different bytes. |
| `axis` | enum | `"+z"` |  | `+x`, `-x`, `+y`, `-y`, `+z`, `-z` |  | Which local axis maps onto the direction. Default '+z' — the forward axis assets face in the examples (a spline-fence style tangent yaw). For ±x/±z the local +Y follows the up hint; for ±y the local +Z follows it. |

## packBricks

Reads axis-aligned BOXES on a stud lattice — one per point, its corners in two point attributes — and writes the BRICKS that build them: one point per brick, from a stated catalogue of footprints, that together fill exactly the lattice cells the boxes cover. The lattice is `pitch` in world units per cell (a stud is 1 across, a plate 0.4 tall, by default), anchored at `origin`; x and z count studs and y counts plates. Every corner must land on the lattice, and one that does not is refused naming the point and the corner, because a box half a stud off is a box the graph upstream built on a different lattice. Boxes may overlap: their cells are unioned, and a cell two boxes claim belongs to the one with the lower point IDENTITY (stored position bits plus `seed`), never the lower array index, so shuffling the input changes nothing — unless two overlapping boxes share both P and `seed`, which identity cannot tell apart, and then the earlier one in the input wins. WHAT A BRICK MAY NOT CROSS is `groupBy`: cells whose boxes differ in any listed attribute are different material and never share a brick, so a wall's colour changes exactly where its boxes' colour does. Each brick carries every point attribute of the box that owns its anchor cell. THE PACKING IS A SCANLINE GREEDY, course by course from the bottom and within a course in z then x. The first unfilled cell of a scan is always the minimum corner of the brick that covers it, so the scan is complete without backtracking; the choice there is WHICH catalogue entry, either way round. HEIGHT COMES FIRST: the tallest entry that fits is the course, so a wall is laid in full bricks and plates appear only where the space is shorter than a brick. Among footprints of that height the score is the footprint in studs less `stagger` for every stud of joint in the brick's bottom course that sits directly on a seam in the course below, counted only on the joints the placement decides — where the cell across is the same material and not yet filled — a running bond, the reason real walls hold together, charged only where a different brick could have avoided it — PLUS `bond` for every two separate pieces the placement joins into one, which is what keeps a floor or a roof laid over an empty room from coming out as loose plates (pieces as stud clutch holds them; see `islandsAttr`). AHEAD OF THAT SCORE, WITHIN THE HEIGHT, COMES WHAT HOLDS. A brick is gripped only through its top and bottom faces, so a placement with no brick under its footprint and no cell to be filled over it — a plate in the middle of a one-plate floor, over an empty room and under another — is loose in every build order. A placement that would leave a cell beside it with no HELD placement left to cover it STRANDS the cell: whatever brick covered it would be loose too. So each placement is charged the loose bricks it commits the build to — one if it is held by nothing, plus one for every cell it strands — and the fewest wins, then a held placement over one held by nothing, then the score. Both charges only ever decide where a course has cells with nothing under or over them; a wall, or anything standing on what is below it, packs by the score alone. Ties go to the larger footprint, then the earlier catalogue entry, then the entry as listed rather than turned. If nothing fits — a catalogue with no 1x1 plate meeting a one-plate gap — the cell is refused by name rather than left empty, so the output always covers the input exactly. THE HOLD SEARCH answers what that one-placement look cannot see: a trap set two or more placements back, an early brick that took the grip a later cell needed. Wherever a course comes out with a brick held by nothing, that course is laid again with each REGION around such a brick — the course's cells of that material that were unfilled when the course began, joined side to side — packed by an EXACT search for a packing in which every brick is held: depth first over the held placements alone, first in scan order and then from the cell the fewest of them cover, each cell's placements tried by the same score, a placement dropped the moment it leaves a cell of the region with no held placement over it, and a failure going straight back to the latest placement to blame for it. Its first answer is the greedy's own choice wherever that choice holds. It is bounded in STEPS of work, never in time, so the result is the same on every machine and under any load: 2097152 per region and 8388608 per cook, a few milliseconds for the regions a generated house has and some 30 ms at the bound. Where the search in scan order runs out, three more answers are asked, each exact and each bounded in steps of its own (4194304 and 1048576 per region, 16777216 per cook, for the first and last; 1048576 per region and 8388608 per cook, and at most 128 cells, for the second): first two proofs that no held packing exists, before the search from the fewest goes on — a PARITY proof, if no set of held placements covers every cell of the region an odd number of times, which is what rules out a one-plate floor walled round whose middle every plate reaches only by an even length, a 21 x 21 slab among them, and PROBES, cases on one cell: each held placement over it is taken in turn with every placement it forces (a cell left exactly one held placement clear of those taken), and if each comes to a cell nothing can cover, or to two forced placements that overlap, no packing exists, which is what rules out a floor over posts too far apart, where a cell only a baseplate reaches leaves another such cell nothing — and, where the search from the fewest runs out as well, a packing by STRAIGHT CUTS — the region's bounding box cut across, again and again, into rectangles each empty or one held catalogue entry — which finds the baseplate-and-strips packings of a big walled slab that a search laying one brick at a time from a corner does not reach. A region proven to have no held packing (a cell no held brick can reach is the usual proof, the parity count and the probes the next most common), one nothing answered within its budget, and one that mixes brick heights are laid by the greedy as before — byte for byte in a course where no region was solved; where one was, its bricks can join pieces the greedy weighs elsewhere in the course. The course is laid again only from the first cell of its new regions, every brick before it kept, and a brick after it is weighed again only where something the greedy reads there — the course's cells round it, the pieces under it — can differ from the laying before; everywhere else the earlier choice stands, so the course comes out brick for brick as laying all of it again would. A course with no brick held by nothing is never searched, so it packs exactly as the greedy alone does. Where the search ran on a course as laid (the regions the ground repair below asks for are its own, and counted there), `out` carries a DETAIL u32 `holdSearch` of four counts — regions searched, solved, proven to have no held packing, and left to the greedy unproven (budget spent, or heights mixed) — and where it never ran there is no such attribute. A BRICK CAN BE HELD AND STILL HANG FROM NOTHING: bricks that hold each other across two or more courses — a floor's edge row under a roof's edge column — with none of them standing on anything that reaches the bottom course, which no look at one course sees. So the finished build is asked what brickConnectivity asks for step -1: the pieces stud clutch makes, and whether each holds a brick of the bottom course. A piece that does not is FLOATING, and is repaired course by course, its lowest first: the build is taken back to that course and laid again from it, with the regions round the piece's bricks there packed by the hold search under one more demand — a brick over a cell of the piece must also cover a cell standing on a brick of a piece that already reaches the bottom course — and every course above laid on that as usual. A repair is kept only if the build it makes, asked again, floats fewer bricks; otherwise the build is put back exactly as it was. A course where a brick of the piece lies in a region the hold search came back from without a held packing is not asked (a firm packing is a held one). Each course asked costs every cell from it up, against a budget of 1048576 cells per cook, never time. Where something floated, `out` carries a DETAIL u32 `groundRepair` of six counts — floating pieces and their bricks before the repair, the same after it (as asked of the finished build, never assumed), courses asked, and repairs kept — and where nothing floated there is no such attribute and the build is packed exactly as without the repair. OUTPUT `out`: one point per brick in scan order. P is the brick's centre, `rot` a quarter turn about y when the entry was placed turned (identity otherwise), and `scale` its full extents in its OWN frame — w and d studs and h plates as listed, in world units — so a unit box copied onto these points draws every brick in place. `boundsMin`/`boundsMax` are its world corners, the same convention it reads. `brick` (string) is the entry as `WxDxH`, ready to be spawnInstances' `assetAttr`, and `brickIndex` (i32) is its position in the catalogue — `bricks` followed by `extraBricks`. OUTPUT `studs`: one point per stud left SHOWING — on a brick's top where the lattice cell above is empty — at the stud's base centre on the brick's top face, carrying the brick's attributes and its `brick`/`brickIndex`, plus `brickId` and `brickAbove` (the indices in `out` of the brick it stands on and the brick resting on it, -1 for none; see `studMode`, which can ask for every stud instead), with identity `rot` and unit `scale`, so a stud mesh modelled at world size with its base at the origin spawns straight onto it; the bounds columns are reset to zero. `islandsAttr` optionally writes a DETAIL u32 on `out` counting the pieces the build falls into when only stud CLUTCH holds it — a brick is joined to the bricks sitting on its top face where their footprints overlap, and side contact does not count, so a value above 1 is a build that comes apart when lifted. HALO: none. A brick spans cells, so cooked over part of the boxes it cuts bricks at the part's edge; this belongs on an unbounded level or before a split. Not GPU-eligible: the packing is sequential by construction. The occupancy grid is dense over the boxes' joint extent and refused past 16.7 million cells.

**Category:** point op

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry), `studs` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-pack-bricks.json](../graphs/basics-pack-bricks.json), [examples-brick-house.json](../graphs/examples-brick-house.json) — 2 total (`pcg graphs --node packBricks`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `minAttr` | string | `"boundsMin"` |  |  |  | POINT attribute (f32 or i32, tuple size 3) holding each box's minimum corner in WORLD units — the absolute corner, not an offset from P, which is what setBounds writes. Converted to lattice cells through `pitch` and `origin` and refused, naming the point, unless every component lands on a lattice plane. |
| `maxAttr` | string | `"boundsMax"` |  |  |  | POINT attribute (f32 or i32, tuple size 3) holding each box's maximum corner in world units, on the same terms as `minAttr`. A box with a maximum below its minimum on any axis is refused; one with zero extent on an axis covers no cells and is skipped. |
| `pitch` | vec3 | `[1,0.4,1]` |  |  |  | World size of one lattice cell: [stud, plate, stud]. The default is the proportion of the familiar toy brick, a plate being 0.4 of a stud's width and a full brick three plates. x and z must be EQUAL, because a stud is square and a brick turned a quarter turn has to land on the same lattice it left; every component must be positive. |
| `origin` | vec3 | `[0,0,0]` |  |  |  | World position of a lattice corner. Corners are read as origin + k * pitch for whole k on each axis, so a set of boxes built on integer studs but shifted by half a stud is packed by moving this, not the boxes. |
| `bricks` | numberList | `[2,8,3,2,6,3,2,4,3,2,3,3,2,2,3,1,8,3,1,6,3,1,4,3,1,3,3,1,2,3,1,1,3,2,16,1,2,12,1,2,10,1,2,8,1,2,6,1,2,4,1,2,3,1,2,2,1,1,12,1,1,10,1,1,8,1,1,6,1,1,4,1,1,3,1,1,2,1,1,1,1]` |  |  |  | The CATALOGUE, as a flat list of [w, d, h] triples: w studs along x, d studs along z, h plates tall, all whole numbers of at least 1, in the brick's own frame. Each entry may also be placed a quarter turn about y (d along x, w along z), so [1, 4, 3] and [4, 1, 3] are the same brick and listing both is refused. The ORDER is the last tie-break: two entries of equal height, footprint and seam cost go to the earlier one. The default holds the common full-height bricks (h 3) from 1x1 to 2x8, and plates (h 1) from 1x1 to 2x16. Without [1, 1, 1] some occupancies cannot be covered at all — a gap one plate tall and one stud wide — and the cook is refused at the first such cell rather than leaving it empty. To ADD to this list rather than replace it, leave it alone and use `extraBricks`. |
| `extraBricks` | numberList | `[]` |  |  |  | More catalogue entries, APPENDED to `bricks` — the default list or a given one — in the same flat [w, d, h] form and on the same terms: whole numbers of at least 1, either way round. This is how a graph asks for the default catalogue plus one baseplate, [32, 32, 1], without copying the 81 numbers of the default. Appended entries come AFTER every `bricks` entry, so they lose the last tie-break to it, and their `brickIndex` continues the count: the first is entry bricks.length / 3. AN ENTRY ALREADY IN THE CATALOGUE IS REFUSED, not skipped — one in `bricks` (either way round) or earlier in `extraBricks` — naming both entries, because a brick listed twice is a list that did not say what its author thought it said, and skipping it would move every later `brickIndex` silently. Empty (the default) adds nothing. |
| `groupBy` | stringList | `[]` |  |  |  | POINT attributes whose values a brick may not cross: two cells whose owning boxes differ in ANY listed attribute (any type, every component compared exactly; strings by value) never share a brick. Typically the colour and the kind — a window's glass is not one brick with the wall around it. Empty (the default) packs every cell as one material. A name the input does not carry is refused, listing what it does carry. |
| `stagger` | f32 | `3` | >= 0 |  |  | The cost, in studs of footprint, of one stud of JOINT in a placement's bottom course that sits directly on a seam in the course below — a joint edge where the two cells beneath it belong to different bricks. Only the joints the placement DECIDES count — where the cell across is the SAME material and not yet filled, so a different brick could still have bridged it: a joint against another colour, empty space or a brick already standing is one every candidate shares, and charging for it would only favour short bricks. Height is not traded against it — the tallest entry that fits is chosen first — so this decides among footprints of one height. At the default of 3 a 1x8 whose end continues a seam scores 8 - 3 = 5 and loses to an unbroken 1x6 at 6, and a 2x8 with both studs of its end on a seam scores 10 against a 2x6's 12; a 1x2 on a seam (-1) loses to a 1x1 (1). 0 packs largest-first and lets seams run straight up a wall. |
| `bond` | f32 | `16` | >= 0 |  |  | The reward, in studs of footprint, for every two separate PIECES a placement joins into one — pieces as stud clutch alone holds them, every piece already standing on the bottom course counting as one (the table holds those together). A plate laid over an empty room touches nothing below and starts a piece of its own; it is held only if something in a course above bridges it to the rest, and this is what makes the packer choose that bridge. Height is still chosen first and the join is weighed against `stagger` within it, so a wall, where everything below is already standing, packs exactly as with 0. The default of 16 is a 2x8 plate's worth: measured over forty generated houses, 0 left 378 bricks that no build order could attach and 5 houses in one piece; with the longer default plates 220 and 11; adding a bond of 16 brought that to 24 bricks and 34 houses, and no value above it did better than 22. 0 ignores what holds together. |
| `studMode` | enum | `"exposed"` |  | `exposed`, `all` |  | Which studs the `studs` output carries. 'exposed' (the default) is the finished build: only studs with nothing resting on them. 'all' is every stud of every brick's top face, whether or not a brick sits on it — what a PARTIAL build shows, since a stud is covered only once the brick above it is placed. Either way each stud carries `brickId`, the index in `out` of the brick it stands on, and `brickAbove`, the index of the brick resting on it or -1; wire this pin into brickConnectivity's `studs` input and it writes both steps onto each stud — `step`, of the brick it stands on, and `stepAbove`, of the brick resting on it (-1 for none or for one never attached) — so 'stud is showing after step t' is `step` >= 0 and `step` <= t and (`stepAbove` < 0 or `stepAbove` > t). |
| `islandsAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | Optional DETAIL attribute (u32) on `out` receiving how many separate pieces the bricks form when only stud clutch holds them together: a brick joins every brick sitting directly on its top face with an overlapping footprint, and side-by-side contact does not join. 1 is a build that lifts as one piece. Empty (the default) writes nothing. |

## partitionByAttribute

Splits the input into one point cloud per distinct value of an i32, u32, or string point attribute (tuple 1). The output collection holds the groups in order of each value's first occurrence; every group carries all point attributes and is tagged `<name>=<value>` (plus the input's tags) so downstream nodes can route by tag.

**Category:** attribute

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-partition-by-attribute.json](../graphs/basics-partition-by-attribute.json), [basics-foreach-per-group.json](../graphs/basics-foreach-per-group.json), [examples-rig.json](../graphs/examples-rig.json) — 3 total (`pcg graphs --node partitionByAttribute`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `name` | string | `"name"` |  |  |  | Point attribute to partition by. Must be i32, u32, or string with tuple size 1. |

## pathClip

CUTS every polyline where `field` changes sign and keeps the pieces where it is >= 0, as polylines of their own — the path node that changes TOPOLOGY by value. pathRuns, pathScan and runFit write attributes over a path's runs and never cut it; a point filter drops points and the whole path's topology with them; this node cuts the path itself, at the place the value crosses zero rather than at the nearest vertex, so a road clipped by a region ends ON the region's edge. THE RULE: `field` is resolved at every vertex; a vertex whose value is >= 0 is KEPT (exactly 0 counts as kept), one whose value is < 0 is cut away. A segment whose two ends disagree is a CROSSING and gets exactly ONE cut point, found by a bracketed root search on the field along that segment — a secant step, falling back to bisection whenever the secant failed to halve the bracket — which is EXACT FOR A FIELD LINEAR ALONG THE SEGMENT (a plane, a slab face, an arc coordinate) and converges to a sign change for any other. The cut point is the last position the search found on the KEPT side, so every emitted point evaluates >= 0. A segment whose ends AGREE is never searched, so a field that dips below zero and back between two vertices is not seen: that is the vertex sampling's resolution, and pathResample first is the fix. A RUN is a maximal kept piece: [entry cut] + consecutive kept vertices + [exit cut]. A cut that lands exactly on its kept vertex is not emitted a second time, so a vertex at exactly 0 ends its run there. A run with fewer than 2 points, or whose length is < `minLength`, is DROPPED. CLOSED PATHS: a closed path with every vertex kept comes back CLOSED and whole; one with none kept disappears; otherwise every run is OPEN, and a run may CROSS THE SEAM — the closing segment is an ordinary segment of the ring, so a piece straddling the start is one run and not two. ORDER is deterministic: runs follow their source polylines in primitive order, and within one polyline they are ordered by the walk index of their first KEPT vertex (on a closed path the run that crosses the seam is therefore the one whose first kept vertex comes latest). WHAT THE POINTS CARRY: a kept vertex is copied with every point attribute it had, its seed included; a cut point takes P from the search and every f32 column LERPED between its segment's two ends at the cut, while integer, bool and string columns come from the segment's KEPT end — and its `seed` is re-drawn from this node's seed, the source primitive and the segment index, so a cut point never shares its kept neighbour's identity. (A lerped `rot` is not renormalised.) A point visited by two polylines is emitted once per run that visits it. Vertex attributes are not carried. PRIMITIVE ATTRIBUTES ARE CARRIED to every run cut from their polyline (primtype excepted, as everywhere), so a clipped road stays a road; DETAIL attributes are carried unchanged. Points on no polyline, and primitives that are not polylines, do not reach the output. OPT-IN REPORTS: `runIndexAttr` (i32 primitive: which surviving run of its source polyline this is, 0, 1, ...), `lengthAttr` (f32 primitive: the run's length) and `arcAttr` (f32 point: distance along the run from its first point). An input with points but no polyline is refused, as every path node refuses one; an input with no points cooks to an empty output. DETERMINISM is structural: nothing is random beyond the seeded cut-point identities, and each crossing's search reads only its own segment. Topology downstream is as fragile as everywhere: a node that can REMOVE points turns these runs back into a cloud.

**Category:** filter

**Inputs:** `in` (geometry)

**Outputs:** `out` (geometry)

**Examples:** [basics-clip-a-path.json](../graphs/basics-clip-a-path.json) — 1 total (`pcg graphs --node pathClip`)

**Params:**

| Param | Type | Default | Range | Enum | Field | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `field` | f32 | `0` |  |  | yes | The signed value that decides what is kept: >= 0 is kept, < 0 is cut away. A plain number keeps everything (>= 0) or nothing (< 0), so this param only means something as a field — a half-space is dot(sub(position(), p0), n), a slab is sub(h, abs(sub(component(position(), 1), y0))), a ball is sub(r, length(sub(position(), c))), a window along a path is a comparison of an arc coordinate written by pathScan. RESOLVED ON POINTS, WITH POSITION FREE: at every input vertex it is resolved on the input itself; during the root search it is resolved on PROBE points the node builds, one per unsettled crossing, standing on the crossing segment — each probe carries exactly what the cut point there would carry (f32 columns lerped, the cut's own seed, the rest from the kept end), so attribute() reads the value AT the probe, randomField draws what it will draw on the emitted cut, and a position-only field sees only position. The two agree exactly at the vertices. `fraction` and `index` are REFUSED by name, because on a probe they measure the batch of crossings being searched rather than any place on the path. A field built with the code combinators carries no spec and cannot be screened; keeping it free of those two is then the author's promise. NaN or ±Infinity anywhere is refused, naming this param: a value that could not be computed says nothing about which side of a boundary it is on. The search is EXACT for a field linear along a segment and converges to a sign change for any other; for a noise, sample the path densely first (pathResample) — a sign change between two agreeing vertices is invisible to it. |
| `minLength` | f32 | `0` | >= 0 |  |  | Runs SHORTER than this, in world units along the run, are dropped — the stubs a boundary leaves where it grazes a path. 0 (the default) keeps every run of at least two points; a run of ONE point is never a polyline and is dropped whatever this says. Measured on the emitted run, cut points included, over the f32 positions it will carry, so it is the length a downstream node measures; a closed path kept whole is measured round its closing segment too. Must be finite and >= 0. |
| `runIndexAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | OPT-IN REPORT: name of an i32 PRIMITIVE attribute receiving each run's ordinal among the runs cut from its source polyline — 0, 1, 2 ... in emission order (walk order of each run's first kept vertex), counting only runs that survived `minLength`, so the numbers are dense. It answers 'which piece of this road am I', which the carried primitive attributes cannot, since every piece of one polyline carries the same ones. A reporting slot: an existing primitive column of the same name and shape (a carried one included) is reset; one of another shape is refused rather than deleted. May not equal `lengthAttr`. Empty (the default) writes none. |
| `lengthAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | OPT-IN REPORT: name of an f32 PRIMITIVE attribute receiving each run's LENGTH in world units, measured as `minLength` measures it — on the emitted f32 positions, the closing segment included for a path kept whole and closed. A reporting slot with the rules `runIndexAttr` states. Empty (the default) writes none. |
| `arcAttr` | string | `""` |  |  |  | OPT-IN REPORT: name of an f32 POINT attribute receiving each point's distance along its run from the run's FIRST point, in world units — 0 at every run's start (an entry cut, or the path's own first kept vertex), the run's length at its last point. It restarts per run, so it composes with pathPointAt's 'distance' mode, transferAlongPath and arcTile on each piece. On a closed path kept whole it reads from the path's first point and leaves out the closing segment, which ends where the coordinate is already 0. A reporting slot checked against the OUTPUT'S point columns — the input's, carried across — so a same-shape column is reset and another shape is refused. Empty (the default) writes none. |

## pathCoverage

Measures, at every point of the `path` input, whether that point is COVERED from some direction by any of the oriented boxes in the `boxes` input, and writes the answer as point attributes: coveredAttr gets a bool (did at least minHits rays hit anything), hitsAttr gets the u32 count of rays that did. The test is a FAN OF REAL RAYS IN WORLD SPACE — rayCount segments running from `near` to `far` along `direction`, offset evenly across the path over -spread..+spread — intersected against each box as an oriented box (position from P, orientation from rot, extent from boxSize times scale, the same columns spawnInstances reads), by the slab method in the BOX'S OWN frame. It casts rays rather than testing path-relative bounds because a bounds projection onto a folded centreline cannot tell 'above the path here' from 'near the path twice': that mistake put a single object near a hairpin in credit for 78 half-widths of lap on 6 half-widths of geometry, and the 32.3% figure it produced was simply wrong. Three cheaper proxies for the same question disagreed with each other by a factor of six; the ray cast is the one that converges as the far plane moves, because a fold is two different places in the world and one place in arc length. A FAR PLANE IS LOAD-BEARING: with no ceiling the sky is a tunnel, so `far` is a real distance and not a sentinel for infinity. Rays are counted DISTINCT, not per box — three boxes stacked over the same two rays is two hits, not six, because the question is whether cover spans the corridor and not how much of it there is. THIS NODE ADDS A COLUMN AND REMOVES NOTHING: points, vertices, primitives, the polyline topology and every existing attribute come through untouched, so a path goes in and the same path comes out one column wider.
